[Docs: What a mod pack is]

# Docs: a topic's title
docs.modPack.title = What a mod pack is

# Docs: the line under the topic's title
docs.modPack.summary = The whole idea, before any of the detail.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.modPack.packOneFolderEverything = A pack is one folder. Everything you author ends up in a single file inside it called modpack.json, and the images and audio sit beside that file.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.modPack.neverEditModpackJson = You never edit modpack.json by hand. The editor writes it, and the tabs across the top are the things it can hold.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.modPack.exportTurnsFolderInto = Export turns the folder into one .smspack file. That is what you hand to players, and the only thing the game reads.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.modPack.gameDoesNotKnow = The game does not know the editor exists. It reads your pack through the SMSModForge plugin, which is what actually builds your characters, rooms and conversations inside the running game.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.modPack.heading.worthKnowing = Worth knowing

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.modPack.packAddsGameRather = A pack adds to the game rather than replacing it. Your places sit alongside the game's own, and a vanilla extension adds to a room the game already has instead of swapping it out.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.modPack.nothingDoEditorTouches = Nothing you do in the editor touches your game installation until you export and copy the pack in yourself.

[Docs: The order to work in]

# Docs: a topic's title
docs.orderWork.title = The order to work in

# Docs: the line under the topic's title
docs.orderWork.summary = Which tabs depend on which, so you are not blocked halfway through.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.orderWork.fileThenNewPack.term = First

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.orderWork.fileThenNewPack = File then New pack, and Save straight away. Previews and file pickers resolve paths against the pack folder, so much of the editor stays empty until that folder exists on disk.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.orderWork.charactersThenNpcsCharacter.term = Who

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.orderWork.charactersThenNpcsCharacter = Characters, then NPCs. A character can speak; an NPC just stands in a room. Both want their art before anything else can point at them.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.orderWork.placesThenMapButtons.term = Where

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.orderWork.placesThenMapButtons = Places, then Map Buttons. A place is a room. A map button is how a player reaches one.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.orderWork.dialoguesDialogueBelongsLevel.term = What they say

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.orderWork.dialoguesDialogueBelongsLevel = Dialogues. A dialogue belongs to a level and its lines name characters, so both are worth having first.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.orderWork.scenesMusicSFXWallpapers.term = What plays

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.orderWork.scenesMusicSFXWallpapers = Scenes, Music, SFX and Wallpapers. These are things a dialogue or a rule can trigger, so add them before the logic that fires them.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.orderWork.variablesThenIntegrationVariable.term = State and logic

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.orderWork.variablesThenIntegrationVariable = Variables, then Integration. A variable has to exist before a condition or an action can name it.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.orderWork.validateF5FixLists.term = Last

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.orderWork.validateF5FixLists = Validate (F5), fix what it lists, then Export.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.orderWork.heading.worthKnowing = Worth knowing

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.orderWork.npcDefinedOnceNpcs = An NPC is defined once on the NPCs tab and then placed into a room from the Places tab, so you will come back to Places after making one.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.orderWork.nothingEnforcesOrderSimply = Nothing enforces this order. It is simply the order that avoids pointing at things you have not made yet.

[Docs: How things point at each other]

# Docs: a topic's title
docs.thingsPointEachOther.title = How things point at each other

# Docs: the line under the topic's title
docs.thingsPointEachOther.summary = The four ways one part of a pack names another, and where each turns up.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.thingsPointEachOther.mostReferencesJustOther.term = By runtime name

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.thingsPointEachOther.mostReferencesJustOther = Most references are just the other item's runtime name: a dialogue node names a character, a placement names an NPC, a condition names a variable.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.thingsPointEachOther.anywhereFieldMeansRoom.term = By place token

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.thingsPointEachOther.anywhereFieldMeansRoom = Anywhere a field means a room or a level. vanilla:Name is one of the game's own levels; place:Key and self:Key are both a place your pack defines, spelt place: where the field means which level this belongs to and self: where it means where to go; pack:PackId.Key reaches a place in somebody else's pack.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.thingsPointEachOther.anywhereFieldMeansOne.term = By hierarchy path

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.thingsPointEachOther.anywhereFieldMeansOne = Anywhere a field means one particular object inside a room, written with arrows, as in NPCs > Shower > Anis > Naked. A path rather than a bare name because names repeat all over a level, and a bare name would find whichever copy the game happened to reach first.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.thingsPointEachOther.artAudioRelativePack.term = By file path

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.thingsPointEachOther.artAudioRelativePack = Art and audio, relative to the pack folder.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.thingsPointEachOther.heading.worthKnowing = Worth knowing

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.thingsPointEachOther.pickAllTheseDropdowns = You pick all of these from dropdowns, but the boxes are editable and show the raw token once you have picked. That is expected: the token is what gets written to the pack, and the friendly name only lives in the list.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.thingsPointEachOther.validateFlagsTokenDoes = Validate flags a token that does not resolve and suggests near matches, which is usually enough to spot a typo or something renamed underneath it.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.thingsPointEachOther.renamingItemLeavesEverything = Renaming an item leaves everything pointing at its old name. Variables are the one exception, where the rename is applied everywhere at once.

[Docs: Putting values into text]

# Docs: a topic's title
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.title = Putting values into text

# Docs: the line under the topic's title
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.summary = Showing a variable inside a line, a label, or another value.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.dialogueTextButtonLabels.term = [PV:name]

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.dialogueTextButtonLabels = Replaced with one of your pack's variables, in any text a player reads: dialogue lines, character names, quest titles, descriptions and tasks, button labels and the text on your screens. It keeps up as the value changes - except in a dialogue line, where it is read as the conversation is built.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.dialogueTextPlayerS.term = {PC}

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.dialogueTextPlayerS = The player's name — whatever they chose at the start. Like the other words in braces here, it works in any text a player reads: dialogue lines, character names, quest titles, descriptions and tasks, button labels and the text on your screens.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.playerCallsTheirMother.term = {M} / {D} / {B}

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.playerCallsTheirMother = What this player calls their mother, father and brother — Anna, Josef and Adrian. Players pick those words themselves, so the same line reads Mum for one and Mom for another. {B} works both ways: Adrian says it back to the player.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.wordChildFamilyS.term = {S} / {DA}

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.wordChildFamilyS = The word for a child of the family: {S} is what Anna and Josef call the player and Adrian, {DA} is what Mario calls Emma.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.playerCallsFamily.term = {F}

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.playerCallsFamily = What this player calls the family.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.writeTheseRatherThan = Write these rather than the words. A line that says Mom says Mom to everybody, including the player who chose something else, and there is no way for them to tell it apart from the game getting it wrong.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.thoseSevenOnesKnown = These are the game's own: a list of words in its dialogue settings, each filled in as the text is shown, bold or coloured if the game says so. Those seven are the ones known to be on it. A word in braces that is not on the list is shown exactly as you typed it.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.actionConditionValueBox.term = $name

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.actionConditionValueBox = In an action or condition value box, stands for that variable's current value instead of a fixed one.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.literalDollarSignRare.term = $$

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.literalDollarSignRare = A literal dollar sign, for the rare case you need one.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.integrationRuleRepeatsOver.term = {item}

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.integrationRuleRepeatsOver = In an integration rule that repeats over a list, stands for whichever value the rule is running for.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.heading.markupLine = Markup in a line

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.setsTextSizeRest.term = <size=70%>

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.setsTextSizeRest = Sets the text size for the rest of the line, as a percentage or a number. The game writes this in its own dialogue — a muttered aside, usually — so it is known to render in the speech bubble.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.boldItalicColouredText.term = <b> / <i> / <color=#FF6666>

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.boldItalicColouredText = Bold, italic and coloured text. These are TextMeshPro's, which is what draws the bubble, and the fact that <size> works at all means markup is parsed rather than printed. But the game never uses these three in its own dialogue, so nobody here has watched one render — try a line before you rely on one.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.writtenRRGGBBRRGGBBAATransparency.term = Colours

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.writtenRRGGBBRRGGBBAATransparency = Written #RRGGBB, #RRGGBBAA with transparency, the short #RGB and #RGBA, or one of ten names: red, lightblue, blue, grey, black, green, white, orange, purple, yellow. Those are the forms the game's text engine reads; anything else leaves the words their ordinary colour. The Colour button picks one for you and writes six digits.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.formattingButtonsOverText = The formatting buttons over the Text box write these for you: Bold (Ctrl+B), Italic (Ctrl+I), Colour (Ctrl+Shift+C) and Size (Ctrl+Shift+S). Each one is a single step for Ctrl+Z.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.tagNotUnderstoodShown = A tag that is not understood is shown to the player exactly as you typed it, the same way a token in the wrong place is. If a line reads <b> in game, that is this.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.heading.worthKnowing = Worth knowing

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.twoSeparateBracedToken = The two are separate. A braced token cannot see a pack variable, and [PV:name] does not resolve the game's names.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.conditionActionCanStill = A condition or action can still read and write the game's own variables — that is the Source toggle on a Variable row. It is only the text token that is one-sided.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.puttingValuesIntoText.variableHasNeverBeen = A variable that has never been set reads as empty. That usually means nothing happens, rather than an error.

[Docs: When something does not work]

# Docs: a topic's title

# Docs: the line under the topic's title
docs.whenSomethingDoesNotWork.summary = The three things to check, in the order worth checking them.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.whenSomethingDoesNotWork.everyCheckAcrossWhole.term = Validate (F5)

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.whenSomethingDoesNotWork.everyCheckAcrossWhole = Every check across the whole pack, listed on this tab. Double-click a row to jump to whatever it is complaining about. Start here: most problems are a missing file or a name that no longer resolves.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.whenSomethingDoesNotWork.theyComposeArtExactly.term = The previews

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.whenSomethingDoesNotWork.theyComposeArtExactly = They compose your art exactly as the game lays it out, but they do not run the game's logic. Conditions, variables and rules are not evaluated, so anything gated on state is drawn regardless — a preview looking right does not mean the gate works.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.whenSomethingDoesNotWork.flagDialogueConditionDebugging.term = In-game logging

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.whenSomethingDoesNotWork.flagDialogueConditionDebugging = Flag a dialogue for condition debugging on the Dialogues tab, or a rule for decision logging on the Integration tab, and the game will log why it did or did not fire.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.whenSomethingDoesNotWork.heading.worthKnowing = Worth knowing

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.whenSomethingDoesNotWork.packDoesNotAppear = A pack that does not appear at all is usually installed wrong rather than broken. The .smspack belongs in BepInEx/plugins/SMSModForge/ModPacks/ inside your game folder.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.whenSomethingDoesNotWork.emptyPreviewPackHave = An empty preview on a pack you have not saved yet is expected. Previews resolve paths against the pack folder.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.whenSomethingDoesNotWork.bothLoggingFlagsStored = Both logging flags are stored in the pack, so tracking left switched on ships to players as extra logging. Clear them before a release.

docs.packsSmspack.heading.worthKnowing = Worth knowing

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.packsSmspack.becausePathsInsideArchive = Because paths inside the archive match the folder exactly, moving a sprite and re-exporting is all it takes — nothing needs re-pointing.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.packsSmspack.packOwnsWholeFolder = A pack owns its whole folder. Export bundles everything inside it, subfolders included, whether the pack refers to it or not — so give a pack a folder of its own rather than saving it into somewhere like your Desktop, which would put every file there into the exported pack.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.packsSmspack.versionControlFoldersEditor = Version-control folders, editor clutter and any earlier .smspack sitting in the folder are left out.

# Docs: a point in the topic

docs.maskPainter.makesOpacitySliderCeiling.term = Cap at opacity

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.maskPainter.makesOpacitySliderCeiling = Makes the opacity slider a ceiling instead of a strength: strokes build up towards it and stop, and anywhere already stronger is left alone. The way to even out a patch without flattening it.

# Docs: a point in the topic

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.characters.pronouns.term = Pronouns

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.characters.pronouns = Male, Female or Neutral: how the character is spoken of. Nothing in English changes, but a translation does - "I'm ready" from a woman is "Estoy lista" in Spanish, and a translator that is not told guesses male. The Translate window tells it who says each line and who each name is. Every character of the pack's own needs one; until it has, the validation list says so. The player's and the game's characters' are the game's, and shown greyed.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.whenSeveralDialoguesBecome = When several dialogues become eligible at the same moment, the highest priority starts first.

docs.dialogues.heading.starts = How it starts

docs.dialogues.hasTrueDialoguePlay.term = Start conditions

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.holdBackRoomS.term = Prioritize this dialogue over vanilla

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.holdBackRoomS = Hold back the room's own entry conversation while yours is eligible, so the two do not compete. Only offered in rooms that have one.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.playArrivalUsualStay.term = Also leave on the Talk button (replayable)

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.playArrivalUsualStay = Play on arrival as usual, and stay on the Talk button so the player can hear it again for as long as its conditions hold. Without this, a dialogue plays once and is gone until its conditions fail and pass again.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.doNotPlayArrival.term = Wait for Talk button (no auto-play)

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.doNotPlayArrival = Do not play on arrival at all — park behind the Talk button and let the player start it. Unavailable while the replay option is on, since that one plays on arrival by definition.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.ifSomethingElsePlaying.term = Queue behind active dialogue

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.ifSomethingElsePlaying = If something else is playing when this becomes eligible, wait and start straight afterwards. Without it, this dialogue misses that moment and waits for the next one.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.gameF12LogsWhich.term = Set for condition debugging (F12)

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.gameF12LogsWhich = In game, F12 logs which start conditions passed and failed, with the values behind them. Diagnostic only — it changes nothing about play.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.dialogues.heading.gameSConversations = The game's conversations

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.gameChecksBeforePlays.term = The game plays this when

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.gameChecksBeforePlays = What the game checks before it plays the conversation, one group per place that plays it - any one of them is enough. Take out removes one of the game's conditions from that place's script; it stays listed, struck through, with Put back beside it. The script is the room's, not the conversation's, so anything else it guards - another conversation, a quest it starts - happens without the condition too, and the Quests tab shows the same change. Where the place is (the room it belongs to) cannot be taken out here - a conversation is always played somewhere; a quest's place on the Quests tab can. A room tries its conversations in order and plays the first one that can, so the ones it says it tries first still come before this one, whatever you take out. Reset all puts these back as well.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.lineOneGameS.term = Saves under way

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.lineOneGameS = A line of one of the game's conversations that you change, add or take out can set or skip something the game's story needs, so it can break a save already under way - and removing your pack later can too. The Dialogues tab says so in orange. The first time a player loads an existing save with a pack that does this, the game warns them and recommends a new game; new games are not warned. A conversation you picked but left as the game has it changes nothing and is not warned about.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.dialogues.heading.nodes = Nodes

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.dialogueTreePlayStarts = A dialogue is a tree. Play starts at the first root and works down: a node runs, then its children in order, until something exits.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.addNodeTopLevel.term = + Root

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.addNodeTopLevel = Add a node at the top level, with no parent.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.addChildSelectedNode.term = + Child

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.addChildSelectedNode = Add a child of the selected node. The speaker, expression, outfit, timing, jump and the whole conditions and actions lists all carry over, so writing the next line of a scene is not a matter of re-picking all of it.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.sameButBesideSelected.term = + Sibling

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.sameButBesideSelected = The same, but beside the selected node rather than under it. It lands directly below what you had selected, not at the end of the row.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.textTagNeverCarry = The text and the tag never carry over. The text is the one thing that is always different, and two nodes sharing a tag would make every jump aimed at the first one ambiguous.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.deleteSelectedNodeAnything.term = − Remove

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.deleteSelectedNodeAnything = Delete the selected node. Anything pointing at it is tidied up for you.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.copyNodeEverythingBeneath = Copy the node and everything beneath it.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.whereCopyLandsBeside.term = Paste below (sibling) / Paste as child / Paste as new root

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.whereCopyLandsBeside = Where the copy lands: beside the selected node, inside it, or out at the top level on its own.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.dialogues.heading.selectedNode = The selected node

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.textPlaysLineChoice.term = Kind

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.textPlaysLineChoice = Text plays a line. Choice offers the children and lets the player pick one. Random picks one of them itself.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.whoSpeakingTheyLook.term = Actor, Expression, Outfit

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.whoSpeakingTheyLook = Who is speaking and how they look, from this node onward. Outfit also accepts $variableName, so a node can pick the outfit from a variable. Expression and Outfit disappear when the player is the speaker: the player's bust belongs to the game, so a pack has neither to offer.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.lineItselfPVName.term = Text

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.lineItselfPVName = The line itself. [PV:name] tokens are filled in as it displays.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.optionalLabelSoAnother.term = Tag

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.optionalLabelSoAnother = An optional label so another node can jump here. Give at most one node any given tag.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.wherePlayGoesAfter.term = Jump

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.wherePlayGoesAfter = Where play goes after this node. Continue, the default, moves to the next node down the list — whether that is a child of this one or something further out. Exit stops the conversation and skips everything below. Jump resumes at whichever node carries the tag you name.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.whichTaggedNodeResume.term = Jump to

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.whichTaggedNodeResume = Which tagged node to resume at. Only appears once Jump is the mode, since Continue and Exit have nowhere to go, and it lists the tags in this dialogue — still typeable, so you can aim at a tag before writing the node that carries it.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.veryBottomDialogueContinue = At the very bottom of a dialogue, Continue and Exit do the same thing, because there is nothing left to skip either way. Exit only means something when something comes after it.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.untilinteractionWaitsPlayerTimeout.term = Duration

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.untilinteractionWaitsPlayerTimeout = UntilInteraction waits for the player; Timeout moves on by itself once the line has finished typing.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.longFinishedLineLingers.term = Timeout (s)

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.longFinishedLineLingers = How long the finished line lingers before it advances. Only appears when Duration is Timeout, since it means nothing otherwise.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.dialogues.heading.perNodeConditionsActions = Per-node conditions and actions

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.dialogues.skipNodeWhenThey.term = Conditions (gate this node)

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.skipNodeWhenThey = Skip the node when they fail. Beneath a Choice, a child that fails is simply not offered to the player.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen

docs.dialogues.runLineBegins = Run as the line begins.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.dialogues.runLineEndsUsual = Run as the line ends — the usual home for anything that should not take effect until the player has read it.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen

# Docs: a point in the topic

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.sfx.hearDefaultVolumeNeeds.term = Play / Stop

docs.sfx.hearDefaultVolumeNeeds = Hear it at the default volume. Needs the pack saved first.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.sfx.heading.autoTriggerPatterns = Auto-trigger patterns

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.sfx.commaSeparatedListText = A comma-separated list of text patterns. When one of them appears in a line, the effect fires on its own — no action needed.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.sfx.conventionLowercaseWordBetween = The convention is a lowercase word between asterisks, like *plap*. One effect can carry several patterns, so different words can share a sound.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.sfx.leaveBlankEffectShould = Leave it blank for an effect that should only ever play when an action asks for it.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.sfx.heading.variants = Variants

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.sfx.effectCanHaveSeveral = An effect can have several recordings, and one is picked at random each time it plays, so a repeated sound does not become monotonous.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.sfx.thereNoFieldThem = There is no field for them: put the extra files beside the first one with the same name and _1, _2, _3 on the end. Audio/Plap.ogg picks up Audio/Plap_1.ogg and Audio/Plap_2.ogg by itself.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.sfx.numberThemWithoutGaps = Number them without gaps. Counting stops at the first missing number, so _1, _2, _4 quietly loads only the first two.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.sfx.extensionsDoNotHave = The extensions do not have to match, so a WAV variant of an OGG effect is fine.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.sfx.playAbovePreviewsFirst = Play above previews the first recording only — the random pick happens in game.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.variables.boolYesNoInt = Bool for yes-or-no, Int and Float for numbers, String for text, List for an ordered set of values.

docs.variables.startsBeforeAnythingHas.term = Default value

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.variables.startsBeforeAnythingHas = What it starts at before anything has set it. Int and Float only accept numbers here; String takes any text.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.variables.boolHasOnlyTwo.term = Starts true / Starts false

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.variables.boolHasOnlyTwo = A Bool has only the two possible values, so it gets this pair of choices in place of the value field.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.variables.listEntriesStartsOut.term = + Add value

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.variables.listEntriesStartsOut = For a List, the entries it starts out holding.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.variables.savePlayerSGame.term = Persisted

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.variables.savePlayerSGame = Save it with the player's game so it survives closing and reopening. Left off, it starts fresh every session — right for anything that only matters within one visit.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.variables.noteYourselfShownOnly.term = Description

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.variables.noteYourselfShownOnly = A note to yourself, shown only in the editor.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.variables.heading.refreshing = Refreshing

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.variables.neverLeavesVariableAlone.term = Refresh mode

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.variables.neverLeavesVariableAlone = Never leaves the variable alone. Daily resets it to its default each in-game day. DailyRandom gives it a fresh random number each day. LevelRandom re-rolls it every time the player enters one particular place.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.variables.whichPlaceReRolls.term = Refresh on level

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.variables.whichPlaceReRolls = Which place re-rolls it, for LevelRandom.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.variables.limitsNumberGameKeeps.term = Min value / Max value

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.variables.limitsNumberGameKeeps = Limits for a number: the game keeps every write inside them. They also set the range the random refresh modes roll within, which is 0 to 100 if you leave them blank.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.variables.heading.worthKnowing = Worth knowing

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.variables.dailyRefreshHappensWhen = A daily refresh happens when the player sleeps, at the point the new day actually begins.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.variables.seePuttingValuesInto = See Putting values into text for the ways a value can be used once it is declared.

# Docs: a point in the topic

# Docs: a point in the topic

# Docs: a heading inside the topic

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.integration.swapPickersJSONView = Swap the pickers for a JSON view of this rule's conditions and actions. The two views round-trip losslessly, so you can move between them freely; a parse error keeps you in code mode until it is fixed.

[Docs: Quests]

# Docs: a topic's title
docs.quests.title = Quests

# Docs: the line under the topic's title
docs.quests.summary = Quests of your own, listed in the game's journal beside the game's.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.quests.heading.twoLists = Two lists

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.questsOwnFoldersCan.term = Quests

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.questsOwnFoldersCan = Quests of your own, in folders you can arrange. + Quest adds one.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.changesGameSOwn.term = Vanilla quests

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.changesGameSOwn = Your changes to the game's own quests, listed apart because the quests are the game's: they are named as the game names them, and folders mean nothing to them. + Vanilla adds one, and each shows how much it changes - tasks added, tasks taken out, and other changes. Each list's − Remove only removes from its own list.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.quests.heading.quest = The quest

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.journalCallsTextBrackets.term = Title

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.journalCallsTextBrackets = What the journal calls it. Text in brackets is painted pink by the journal itself, the way the game writes its own.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.questActionsConditionsName.term = Runtime name

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.questActionsConditionsName = What Quest actions and conditions name it by. It follows the title while the quest is new; Rename changes it afterwards and every action and condition naming it follows.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.paragraphUnderTitleJournal.term = Description

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.paragraphUnderTitleJournal = The paragraph under the title in the journal.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.quests.heading.startsWhen = Starts when

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.whenAllThemPass.term = Start conditions

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.whenAllThemPass = When all of them pass, the quest starts by itself and its first task with it. They are checked continuously, so they offer the same conditions a dialogue's start conditions do.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.noStartConditionsQuest.term = Empty

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.noStartConditionsQuest = With no start conditions the quest waits for a Quest action to start it. Unlike most condition lists, empty does not mean always.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.quests.heading.resetsWhen = Resets when

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.whenAllThemPass2.term = Reset conditions

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.whenAllThemPass2 = When all of them pass, a quest that has started - in progress, completed or failed - goes back to not started, its tasks and counts with it, so it can be done again. They are checked continuously. On your quests and on the game's.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.whileResetConditionsPass.term = Start conditions wait

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.whileResetConditionsPass = While the reset conditions pass, the start conditions are not asked, so a quest whose two lists both pass does not start and reset in turn.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.noResetConditionsQuest.term = Empty

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.noResetConditionsQuest = With no reset conditions the quest only goes back to not started when a Quest action resets it - or, for one of the game's, where the game does it.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.quests.heading.stepsAdded = Tasks added after players finished it

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.stepsAdded.leave.term = Leave it finished

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.stepsAdded.leave = The default. A player who had finished the quest keeps it finished and never sees the new tasks.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.stepsAdded.reopen.term = Reopen at the new steps

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.stepsAdded.reopen = The quest is in progress again the next time the player loads their save: every task they did stays done, and the first new task starts. Only when it is a clean addition - every new task comes after every task the quest already had, and the player had done all of those. Otherwise it stays finished, so nobody is stuck behind a task they cannot reach. A new choice under an "any one" task, or a note under a "by action" one, never reopens it.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.stepsAdded.startOver.term = Start it over

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.stepsAdded.startOver = The player's progress on the quest is cleared and it goes back to not started, so it starts again the way it always starts - its start conditions, or a Quest action. Each task's actions run again as it is done.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.stepsAdded.whichNew.term = Which tasks are new

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.stepsAdded.whichNew = ModForge remembers which tasks the quest had the last time each save was played with your pack. A save from before this was remembered has no record, so for it the tasks the player never did that come after the last one they did count as new. Only your own quests: one of the game's quests stays as the player left it.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.quests.heading.tasks = Tasks

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.topLevelTasksRun.term = Order

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.topLevelTasksRun = Top-level tasks run one after another. Starting the quest starts the first, finishing one starts the next, and finishing the last completes the quest.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.listedUnderTheirTask.term = Subtasks

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.listedUnderTheirTask = Listed under their task with a small arrow. The journal never ticks a subtask, finished or not; only top-level tasks get a check mark.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.questSDescriptionBecomes.term = Quest description once done

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.questSDescriptionBecomes = What the quest's description becomes once this task is done. Several tasks can have one: the journal shows the one from the task furthest down the list that is done, and the quest's own description before any of them is.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.subtaskJournalListsEvery.term = Hidden until it starts

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.subtaskJournalListsEvery = On a subtask. The journal lists every subtask of a task it shows, started or not; ticked, this one stays out of the journal until it starts - under a task whose subtasks run in order, once the subtask before it is done. A top-level task needs no tick: the journal already leaves one out until it starts.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.anyTaskKeepsOut.term = Hidden until conditions pass

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.anyTaskKeepsOut = On any task. Keeps it out of the journal until the conditions under it all pass. Once they have, it stays in the journal for the rest of that save - remembered with the save - unless 'Hide it again when they stop passing' is ticked, in which case it is shown only while they pass. With no conditions it is shown at once. Hiding a task changes nothing about when it or its quest finishes.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.taskSubtasksFinishesOrder.term = Completes

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.taskSubtasksFinishesOrder = How a task with subtasks finishes: in order (one subtask at a time), any order (all of them, started together), any one (the first one done), or by action. By action means its subtasks never start, so they read as notes under it.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.givesTaskCountReaching.term = Counts to

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.givesTaskCountReaching = Gives the task a count. Reaching the target completes the task. The journal only shows the count beside a top-level task.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.questActionQuestActions.term = Counted by

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.questActionQuestActions = Quest action: Quest actions set the count or add to it. Variable: the count follows a number variable - one of your pack's, or one of the game's - for as long as the task is in progress, and completes the task when the variable reaches the target.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.conditionsFinishTaskThemselves.term = Completes when

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.conditionsFinishTaskThemselves = Conditions that finish the task by themselves: while it is in progress, the moment all of them pass it is done. With none, only a Quest action or the counter finishes it.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.actionsRunTaskFinishes.term = When it completes

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.actionsRunTaskFinishes = Actions that run as the task finishes, however it was finished: by its conditions, by a Quest action anywhere in the pack, or by its counter.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.onlyTaskWithoutSubtasks = Only a task without subtasks has these two lists. A task with subtasks finishes through them, so its subtasks carry the conditions and actions instead.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.quests.heading.gameSQuests = The game's quests

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.vanillaQuestsListEntry.term = + Vanilla

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.vanillaQuestsListEntry = In the Vanilla quests list: an entry about one of the game's own quests. The quest stays the game's; your pack changes what the journal shows of it, what happens as the player works through it, and - if you choose - which tasks it has. Everything it changes goes back to the game's own if your pack is removed.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.whichGameSQuests.term = Quest

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.whichGameSQuests = Which of the game's quests it is about, by the name the game's own instructions use. The journal title is shown beside it, and the two often differ.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.muchEntryChangesButton.term = Changes and Reset all

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.muchEntryChangesButton = How much the entry changes, and a button that puts the whole quest back the way the game has it - its description and tasks, your tasks in it, your start and reset conditions, and every place that starts or resets it, including conditions taken out of them. The description has a Reset of its own. A task of the game's that you change is marked with a dot in the list; its panel lists each change with its own way back, and Reset puts the task back.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.paragraphQuestShipsSo.term = The game says

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.paragraphQuestShipsSo = The paragraph that quest ships with, so you can see what an override would replace.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.replacesParagraphWhilePack.term = Description

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.replacesParagraphWhilePack = Replaces that paragraph while your pack is installed. Leave it empty to keep the game's own.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.gameSTasksGame.term = Tasks

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.gameSTasksGame = The game's tasks in the game's order, with yours among them marked 'yours'. Faded rows are hidden from the journal; struck-through ones are taken out of the quest.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.addsTopLevelTask.term = + Task

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.addsTopLevelTask = Adds a top-level task of yours just after the selected top-level task, or at the end. The game waits for it like any of its own: the tasks after it hold until it is done, and the quest does not finish without it.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.addsSubtaskYoursEnd.term = + Subtask

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.addsSubtaskYoursEnd = Adds a subtask of yours - at the end of the selected top-level task's subtasks, or just after the selected subtask. Works on the game's steps too, including one with no subtasks yet, which then finishes through its subtasks as the game does. Added tasks go two levels deep, as the game's do.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.moveTaskYoursAmong.term = ▲ ▼

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.moveTaskYoursAmong = Move a task of yours among the tasks it sits with, past the game's. The game's own cannot be moved.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.deletesTaskYoursTakes.term = − Remove

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.deletesTaskYoursTakes = Deletes a task of yours, or takes one of the game's out of the quest.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.selectingOneOpensSame.term = Your tasks

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.selectingOneOpensSame = Selecting one opens the same panel a task of your own quest has: text, runtime name, Quest description once done, Hidden until it starts, Hidden until conditions pass, how it completes, its counter, and its completion conditions and actions. Quest actions and conditions name it on the Vanilla side, by the game's quest and its runtime name.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.oneGameSTasks.term = Take it out of the quest

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.oneGameSTasks = On one of the game's tasks. It is not deleted - the game's own scenes still name it - but hidden, with everything under it, and the quest moves past it the moment it starts, as though the game had completed it. Under a task that finishes with any one of its subtasks it is left alone instead, so it cannot finish that task by itself. Untick to put it back.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.oneGameSTasks2.term = In the journal

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.oneGameSTasks2 = On one of the game's tasks: shown as the game has it, hidden until conditions of yours pass (with the same 'Hide it again when they stop passing' choice a task of your own has), always hidden, or (for a subtask) hidden until it starts. Hiding a task changes nothing about when the quest finishes.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.anyTaskListQuest.term = Quest description once done

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.anyTaskListQuest = On any task in the list: what the quest's description becomes once that task is done. The journal shows the one from the task furthest down the list that is done.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.actionsRunOnceMoment.term = When the game completes it

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.actionsRunOnceMoment = Actions that run once, the moment the game marks one of its tasks done - by its own dialogue or by a Quest action of yours.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.conditionsStartGameS.term = Starts when

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.conditionsStartGameS = Conditions that start the game's quest early, if the game has not started it already. What the game does to start it normally is unchanged.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.readOnlyQuestEach.term = What the game does with it

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.readOnlyQuestEach = Read-only, on the quest and on each of its tasks: every place the game starts the quest or puts it back to not started, and every place it completes, counts, fails or abandons a task. Each says which conversation and line it is on (as the line appears, or once it is done) and the lines leading there, or which script on which object runs it and which conversations that script plays just before and after it, and what has to be true - the conditions on the way and what the conversation needs to play. A condition ModForge has no equivalent for is listed in the game's own words. A step on a task only takes effect while that task is in progress. Every quest step in the game is covered.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.placesUnderStartedPut.term = Changing a place

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.placesUnderStartedPut = On the places under Started by and Put back to not started by. Each of the game's conditions around the step is listed where it lives - a line on the way, the line itself, the place that plays the conversation, or the script. Take out removes one from the game (struck through, with Put back); a line's condition is taken out of your version of that conversation, a place's out of its script, and either way whatever else it guards happens without it too - the Dialogues tab shows the same change. Where the script sits is a row of its own and can be taken out as well. And only if (yours) adds conditions of your own. Once you change anything at a place, that place is yours: what is left - the game's conditions you kept, the room, and yours - is asked over and over, and the quest starts (or goes back to not started) the moment it all passes, whether or not the game's own conversation or script ever gets there. Take everything out and add a key press, and the key press starts it; take the room out too and it starts anywhere. A place you have not touched behaves as the game wrote it, and putting everything back gives it back to the game. A place also says which of the room's conversations the game tries first, which decides the conversation but not your rule, and the game's log says which one the room played. A changed place is marked with a dot, and its reset arrow puts it back the way the game has it. Places that complete, count or fail a task are shown, not changed.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.besidePlaceConversationShows.term = Open conversation

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.besidePlaceConversationShows = Beside a place in a conversation: shows that conversation on the Dialogues tab with the line selected. If your pack does not change it yet, you are asked before it is added to your vanilla conversations; it changes nothing until you edit it.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.everyTaskListGame.term = When a task starts

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.everyTaskListGame = Every task in the list, the game's and yours, says when it starts. Nothing starts a task directly; the quest's order does. The first top-level task starts with the quest and each one after it once the one before is done; a task whose subtasks run in order starts the first of them, one whose subtasks run in any order - or finish with any one - starts them all, and one completed by an action starts none. A task your pack takes out is skipped on the way.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.addingTakingOutTasks.term = Saves under way

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.addingTakingOutTasks = Adding or taking out tasks can leave a save that is part-way through the quest unable to finish it, and so can removing your pack later. The editor says so in orange on any entry that does it. The first time a player loads an existing save with such a pack, the game warns them, lists the quests whose tasks change with where their save is in each - not started, in progress, possibly stuck because a task was added before where they are, or already finished - and recommends a new game; new games are not warned. Changes to the game's conversations are warned about the same way - see Dialogues; changes to its places are not. A save that has progress on tasks a quest no longer has is put back on its feet once: the plugin completes steps whose subtasks are all done, starts the next step where nothing is in progress, and completes the quest when all its steps are.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.quests.loadingSaveHasData.term = Missing packs

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.loadingSaveHasData = Loading a save that has data from a pack that is not installed tells the player which pack, and that its data will not be carried into saves they make from then on.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.quests.heading.worthKnowing = Worth knowing

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.questNeedsNothingOutside = A quest needs nothing outside this tab to start, move along and finish. The Quest action and the QuestState and QuestCounter conditions let a dialogue, a rule or a button take part as well - see Actions and Conditions.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.gameOnlyFinishesTask = The game only finishes a task that is in progress. Completing a task the player has not reached yet does nothing, and says so in the game's log.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.finishingQuestGivesPlayer = Finishing a quest gives the player the same reward the game's own quests give.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.quests.playersProgressSavedUnder = Players' progress is saved under the runtime names of the quest and its tasks. Renaming either after release starts that quest over for everyone who has the pack.

[Docs: Conditions]

# Docs: a topic's title
docs.conditions.title = Conditions

docs.conditions.summary = Everything you can ask about the state of the game.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.conditions.heading.variables = Variables

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.conditions.compareVariableAgainstValue.term = Variable

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.compareVariableAgainstValue = Compare a variable against a value. Source chooses between your pack's own variables and the game's; Comparison offers equals, greater than, greater or equal, less than, less or equal, and exists — which passes once the variable has been given any value at all.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.conditions.passesWhenTextVariable.term = VariableStartsWith

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.passesWhenTextVariable = Passes when a text variable begins with something. The one the comparisons above cannot express: where a value is compound, like a location built from a room and a spot in it, a prefix answers which room am I in without listing every spot.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.conditions.heading.lists = Lists

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.conditions.passesWhenListHolds.term = ListContains

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.passesWhenListHolds = Passes when a list holds a particular value. Negate it for does not contain, which is how you express not already taken.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.conditions.compareManyEntriesList.term = ListCount

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.compareManyEntriesList = Compare how many entries a list holds against a number. Equals zero is the empty test, and negating that gives has something in it.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.conditions.heading.whereThings = Where things are

docs.conditions.passesWhileParticularObject = Passes while a particular object is switched on. It says which object with the same Category and Target row an action uses — see The target picker — because it is reading back exactly what switching something on and off writes.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.conditions.heading.keyboardMouse = Keyboard and mouse

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.conditions.passesKeyMouseButton.term = InputKey

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.passesKeyMouseButton = Passes on a key or a mouse button. Device picks which list you choose from, Key is the key itself, and When is one of four things to ask about it.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.conditions.momentsTrueOncePer.term = Pressed / Released

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.momentsTrueOncePer = Moments. True once per press, on the frame it happens. Only useful where the condition is re-checked continuously — an integration rule, a button's visibility, a dialogue's start conditions.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.conditions.statesTrueLongKey.term = Down / Up

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.statesTrueLongKey = States. True for as long as the key is held, or for as long as it is not. These work anywhere, including a node's own conditions.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.conditions.gameReadsKeysPOSITION.term = Keyboard layouts

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.gameReadsKeysPOSITION = The game reads keys by POSITION, not by the letter printed on the cap — the key called A is wherever A sits on a US keyboard, which is Q on AZERTY. The picker groups the letters, number row and punctuation separately for that reason; mouse buttons, arrows, modifiers, function keys and the numpad sit in the same place everywhere and are the safe choice for anything a player has to be able to press.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.conditions.gameMouseButtonJust.term = Mouse buttons are keys too

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.gameMouseButtonJust = To the game a mouse button is just another key, so there is one Key field and the Device picker only decides which list it offers.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.percentageRolledOncePer = A percentage rolled once per in-game day, which then holds steady for the rest of it. Every use is its own independent gate — two rules at seventy percent are two separate coin flips, not one shared answer.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.passesNoMatterUseful = Passes no matter what. Useful while testing something else.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.conditions.heading.whereEachOneOffered = Where each one is offered

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.mostConditionsAvailableAnywhere = Most conditions are available anywhere a condition can be written.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.randomOfferedOnlyWhere = Random is offered only where a condition is checked once and then done with — a node's own conditions, and a place's enter and exit hooks. Anywhere that is checked continuously, a fresh roll every frame would flicker rather than decide, so DailyChance is the one to reach for.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.timerOfferedOnlyIntegration = Timer is offered only on integration rules, because it measures from the last time the rule fired and nothing else has a moment like that.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.inputkeyOfferedEverywhereBut = InputKey is offered everywhere, but its two edge phases — Pressed and Released — are only meaningful where a condition is re-checked continuously. On a node's own conditions, which are checked once when the conversation reaches the node, Validate warns and points you at Down or Up.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.conditions.heading.theyCalledFile = What they are called in the file

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.onlyNeededReadingModpack = Only needed for reading modpack.json, or an integration rule in Code mode. The picker never shows these names, and there is nothing to type.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.oneVariableEntrySix = The one Variable entry is six types underneath, chosen by the Comparison box: VariableEquals, VariableGreaterThan, VariableGreaterOrEqual, VariableLessThan, VariableLessOrEqual and VariableExists. Source picks between your pack's variables and the game's; it is a parameter, not a different type.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.everythingElseWrittenUnder = Everything else is written under the name the picker shows it under.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.conditions.gamevariableequalsGamevariablenumbergreaterthanGamevariablenumbergreaterorequalGamevariablenumberlessthan.term = Older packs

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.gamevariableequalsGamevariablenumbergreaterthanGamevariablenumbergreaterorequalGamevariablenumberlessthan = GameVariableEquals, GameVariableNumberGreaterThan, GameVariableNumberGreaterOrEqual, GameVariableNumberLessThan and GameVariableNumberLessOrEqual were how a pack used to reach the game's own variables. Opening such a pack rewrites each of them to the matching Variable type with Source set to Vanilla, so they will not survive the next save and there is no reason to write a new one.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.conditions.heading.worthKnowing = Worth knowing

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.anyConditionCanNegated = Any condition can be negated, which usually saves writing the opposite test.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.conditions.seeConditionActionLists = See Condition and action lists for grouping them, and Putting values into text for using one variable's value inside another condition.

docs.targetPicker.summary = The shared row for saying which object to act on, or ask about.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen

# Docs: a point in the topic

docs.targetPicker.somethingPackAddedPlace.term = GameObjects

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.targetPicker.somethingPackAddedPlace = Something your pack added to a place. Pick the place first, then the object within it.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.targetPicker.placeSOwnArtwork.term = Places

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.targetPicker.placeSOwnArtwork = The place's own artwork rather than anything in it.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.targetPicker.oneScenesName.term = Scene

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.targetPicker.oneScenesName = One of your scenes, by name.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.targetPicker.anythingElseNamedWhere.term = Direct Path

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.targetPicker.anythingElseNamedWhere = Anything else, named by where it sits in the game — either a plain object name or a full path like 9_MainCanvas/AfterSleepEvents.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.targetPicker.heading.restRow = The rest of the row

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.targetPicker.actOfferedWhateverCategory.term = Target

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.targetPicker.actOfferedWhateverCategory = What to act on, offered from whatever the category makes available. It also accepts $variableName, so a rule can act on whichever object a variable currently names — useful for hiding whatever was showing without listing every possibility.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.targetPicker.whichPlaceLookInside.term = Level

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.targetPicker.whichPlaceLookInside = Which place to look inside, when the category is GameObjects. Naming it matters while moving between places: it makes sure the object found is the one in the place you meant, not a same-named object still standing in the place being left.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.targetPicker.whichPlaceSTwo.term = Layer

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.targetPicker.whichPlaceSTwo = Which of a place's two pieces of artwork to act on, when the category is Places — Back or Front, the same words the Places tab uses.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.targetPicker.heading.worthKnowing = Worth knowing

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.targetPicker.notEveryActionOffers = Not every action offers every category. Switching something on or off leaves out Places, because activating a whole place is what moving between places is for.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.targetPicker.gameobjectactiveConditionUsesSame = The GameObjectActive condition uses the same row, and offers the same four categories switching something on and off does. Asking whether a whole place is on screen is what LevelActive is for.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.targetPicker.objectsStartSwitchedOff = Objects that start switched off are still found, so an action can turn on something that was never visible.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.targetPicker.targetResolvesNothingReported = A target that resolves to nothing is reported in the game's log rather than stopping anything, so a mistyped name shows up as an action that quietly does nothing.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.targetPicker.conditionThereNoLog = In a condition there is no log line for it: an object that cannot be found reads the same as one switched off. Negate therefore means off or absent, not off — so a mistyped target in a negated condition passes rather than complaining.

[Docs: Validating]

# Docs: a topic's title
docs.validating.title = Validating

# Docs: the line under the topic's title
docs.validating.summary = Checking the pack before it goes anywhere.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.validating.validateF5ChecksWhole = Validate (F5) checks the whole pack and fills the Issues list on the ModForge tab. Double-click a row to jump to whatever it is complaining about.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.validating.errorsThingsWillNot = Errors are things that will not work: a missing file, a required name left blank, a reference to something that no longer exists, two items sharing a key.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.validating.warningsThingsWillRun = Warnings are things that will run but are probably not what you meant — a default value that does not parse as its type, a character set to borrow a bust without one chosen, two placements landing on the same spot.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.validating.referencesPlacesGameShips = References to places the game ships with are checked against the version the editor knows, so a name that changed between game versions is caught here rather than in play.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.validating.heading.worthKnowing = Worth knowing

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.validating.validateRunsAgainstScreen = Validate runs against what is on screen. Save before exporting, since the exporter bundles what is on disk.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.validating.clearAnyDebugTracking = Clear any debug tracking before a release — see The ModForge tab.

docs.exportingInstalling.putSmspackBepinexPlugins.term = Installing

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.exportingInstalling.putSmspackBepinexPlugins = Put the .smspack in BepInEx/plugins/SMSModForge/ModPacks/ inside the game folder. Exporting straight there means testing a change is export, then restart the game.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.exportingInstalling.eachSaveRecordsWhich.term = Game version

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.exportingInstalling.eachSaveRecordsWhich = Each save records which version of the game the pack was built against, and the editor shows that version in its title bar. It travels with the pack, so a pack built for one version can be recognised when the game moves on.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.exportingInstalling.heading.worthKnowing = Worth knowing

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.exportingInstalling.exportUnavailableUntilPack = Export is unavailable until the pack has been saved at least once.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.exportingInstalling.seePacksSmspackEnds = See Packs and the .smspack for what ends up inside the file.

# Docs: a topic's title
docs.translations.title = Translations

# Docs: the line under the topic's title
docs.translations.summary = The editor in your language, and your pack in your players'.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.translations.heading.editor = The editor

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.translations.languageMenu.term = Language menu

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.languageMenu = Lists every language the editor has a translation for. Pick one and the editor switches to it at once, with your pack open just as it was - nothing is closed and nothing is lost. "Windows' language" follows Windows, and is English where there is no translation.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.translations.editMode.term = Edit texts on screen

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.editMode = In the Language menu, in any language but English: a pencil appears beside every text on screen. Click one to see the English and write your own words for it - a text that says a number shows each form the language uses. Saved in your languages folder and shown at once. Texts in menus, messages and tooltips have no pencil: Find a text to edit searches all of them by their words.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.translations.files.term = Translation files

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.files = One text file per language, named for it: es.txt, pt-BR.txt, zh-Hans.txt. Each line is key = text, with the English it was translated from in a note above it. Any text editor opens one, and a Replace All in Notepad++ is safe: Check a translation finds the keys it changed and puts them back.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.translations.newOrUpdate.term = New or update a translation…

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.newOrUpdate = Writes a file of your own to start from, in your languages folder, or brings yours up to date after an update of the editor: new texts arrive in English, changed ones are marked 'changed from', and nothing you wrote is lost.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.translations.check.term = Check a translation…

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.check = Lists what is missing, broken or out of date, line by line: a {gap}, a <tag> or a count form lost in translation, a line the editor cannot read.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.translations.sharing.term = Passing one on

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.sharing = Send the file. Whoever gets it puts it in their languages folder (Language ▸ Open my languages folder), and it appears in their Language menu. A file there is laid over the one that came with the editor: its words show wherever it has some for the English as it is now, and the editor's own translation everywhere else - so an update of the editor still brings its new texts, and never touches the file.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.translations.heading.packs = Your pack in other languages

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.translations.writtenIn.term = Written in

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.writtenIn = On the ModForge tab: the language your pack's own words are in. Translations are made from it, lines are spell-checked in it, and a player whose game is in it gets your words as they are.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.translations.editingIn.term = Editing in

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.editingIn = Beside the tabs: pick a language to see the whole pack in it - lines, names, quests, buttons - and edit it right where you edit your own words. What you type goes into that language's file and your own words stay as they are; saving writes both, and lists the translation's changes with the rest.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.translations.translatePack.term = Translate the pack…

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.translatePack = File ▸ Pack translations ▸ Translate the pack…: translates by machine (Google) whatever is still untranslated, into the languages you tick. Characters' names are kept as they are and spelled in each language's own alphabet - Google suggests, and you check, before anything is translated - and the pronouns you gave your characters tell it who says each line. Everything a machine writes is marked, and Editing in corrects any of it.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.translations.packFile.term = Pack translations

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.packFile = File ▸ Pack translations ▸ New or update a translation… writes translations/<code>.txt in the pack's folder, with every text a player reads: lines and choices, character names, quest titles, descriptions and tasks, navigator and world map buttons, and the texts on your screens. Each has a note saying where it appears. The folder goes into the exported pack.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.translations.ai.term = With an AI tool

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.ai = Give it the whole file and ask it to translate only the text after each =, changing nothing else. Save what it gives back in place of the file, then run File ▸ Pack translations ▸ Check the pack's translations: it finds any key, gap, tag or [PV:...] token that did not come back as it went.

# Docs: the bold label in front of the next text, usually a name on screen
docs.translations.players.term = What players see

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.players = A player whose game is set to that language gets the pack in it, with nothing to set up. A line you change after it was translated shows your new words until the translation is brought up to date, never an old translation of something else.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.translations.heading.game = In the game

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.setting = The plugin's Language setting, in BepInEx\config\treboy.starmakerstory.smsmodforge.packplugin.cfg, picks the language for ModForge's own words and for every pack. auto follows the language Windows is set to.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.fonts = The game itself is in English and stays so. Its fonts only draw Latin letters, so for Chinese, Japanese, Korean or Russian the plugin puts Windows' own font for that language behind them.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.gameWords = ModForge's own words in the game - the pack list on the main menu, the save warning - come from the same translation file as the editor's, under [In the game]. Put the file in BepInEx\plugins\SMSModForge\Languages and players see them in that language.

# Docs: a heading inside the topic
docs.translations.heading.worthKnowing = Worth knowing

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.machine = Translations that came with the editor were made by a machine, and say so in the Language menu until somebody checks them. Corrections are welcome: make them with Edit texts on screen, or in the file, and pass the file on.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.storedStays = What a pack stores does not change with the language: keys, names you gave things and the values conditions compare stay as you typed them.

# Docs: a point in the topic
docs.translations.variables = Text you put into a variable and show with [PV:...] is not translated: it is data, and a condition may be comparing it.
