[Tutorial: First steps]

# Tutorials: a tutorial's name
tutorial.firstSteps.title = First steps

# Tutorials: the line under a tutorial's name
tutorial.firstSteps.summary = Make a pack, put a character in it, and give it to somebody.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstSteps.willEndUp.title = What you will end up with

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstSteps.willEndUp.body = A pack on disk with one character in it, ready for the game to load. Nothing here is throwaway — this is a real pack, and the next tutorial carries on in the same one.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstSteps.startFreshPack.title = Start a fresh pack

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstSteps.startFreshPack.body = Use File then New pack. Starting clean matters here: the steps that follow watch for things appearing, and in a pack you have already been working in some of them are there before you begin.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.firstSteps.startFreshPack.hint = File then New pack. Anything unsaved will be offered back to you first.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstSteps.saveSomewhere.title = Save it somewhere

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstSteps.saveSomewhere.body = Use File then Save, and make a NEW empty folder for it — not your Desktop or Documents. A pack owns its whole folder: everything inside ends up in the exported file. Saving here also matters because previews and file pickers resolve paths against that folder, and stay empty until there is one.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.firstSteps.saveSomewhere.hint = File then Save, or Ctrl+S. Make a new folder rather than reusing one.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstSteps.someArtWork.title = Some art to work with

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstSteps.someArtWork.body = Practice sprites have been copied into a TutorialArt folder inside your pack: busts, a room, a couple of NPCs and a scene. They are yours now — edit them, replace them, or delete the folder when you are done with it.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstSteps.addCharacter.title = Add a character

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstSteps.addCharacter.body = Characters are anyone who speaks. Use + Character, which is the one that draws its own bust rather than borrowing the game's.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.firstSteps.addCharacter.hint = + Character sits in the toolbar above the character list.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstSteps.giveThemName.title = Give them a name

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstSteps.giveThemName.body = This is what players read above a line of dialogue, and the only name you have to write — the identifiers the pack uses internally follow from it.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.firstSteps.giveThemName.hint = Type over the placeholder name in the Identity box.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstSteps.giveThemFace.title = Give them a face

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstSteps.giveThemFace.body = Every character needs at least one outfit, and every outfit needs a base sprite: the picture of them from the chest up. Point this one at one of the five practice busts. They live in TutorialArt/Busts, one folder each, and the base picture is the file whose name ends in Base00 — so Bust1 is TutorialArt/Busts/Bust1/Bust1Base00.png, and Bust2 through Bust5 follow the same pattern.\n\nAny of them works here. They differ in shape on purpose, which will matter when you paint a jiggle mask later; the fuller ones give you more to see. The other files in those folders are the blink, mouth and expression art, and a later tutorial uses them.\n\nBust art is 256 by 256 pixels, PNG, with a transparent background. Other sizes are scaled to fit rather than refused, but 256 is the only size whose pixels land exactly as you drew them, and anything that is not square gets transparent bars on two sides. When you come to draw your own, that is the shape to draw.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.firstSteps.giveThemFace.hint = Use the Browse button beside Base, under Sprites.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstSteps.thereThey.title = There they are

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstSteps.thereThey.body = The preview draws the bust the way the game will, and keeps up as you work — worth watching rather than exporting to find out.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstSteps.sayOutfitHas.title = Say what this outfit has

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstSteps.sayOutfitHas.body = A bust can carry more than its base picture: a blink frame, four mouth frames that animate while it talks, and four expressions. The three tickboxes are how you DECLARE which of those exist — they are not features to switch on, they are you telling the game what art to go looking for.\n\nYou are not using any of them yet, so untick all three: Has Blink frame, Has Mouth frames, Has Expressions. Getting this wrong is worth understanding now, because it fails silently — a ticked box with no art behind it reads as a BROKEN outfit, and the game skips the whole character rather than skipping the blink. A missing character with no error is almost always this.\n\nLeave Mask empty. An empty mask means the bust does not move, which is a perfectly good answer and the one you want until you paint one.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.firstSteps.sayOutfitHas.hint = Two tickboxes in the Sprites box, one in Expressions just below it.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstSteps.pack.title = That is a pack

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstSteps.pack.body = Save it, and File then Export pack bundles the folder into a single .smspack — the file the game loads. Drop that into the Mods folder in your game folder, beside the game's .exe, and it is installed.\n\nExport is the quick loop, for trying your own work in the game. Use it as often as you like: it changes nothing about the pack itself.\n\nThere is nothing to see in the game yet, because a character only appears once something gives them a line.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstSteps.givingSomebodyElse.title = Giving it to somebody else

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstSteps.givingSomebodyElse.body = File then Publish for players is the other end of that. It checks the pack over first and says so if anything is broken, then writes a zip holding one thing: your pack, already sitting in a Mods folder. Whoever downloads it extracts that into their game folder and is finished — there is no step left for them to get wrong, which is where most mods are lost. Nothing else is in the archive, so installing two packs never asks anybody to overwrite anything.\n\nIt opens the folder it wrote to afterwards, so the file is never somewhere you have to go looking for.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstSteps.writtenIn.title = The language it is written in

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstSteps.writtenIn.body = Written in, on this tab, says which language your pack's own words are in. It matters once the pack is translated: translations are made from it, and a player whose language has no translation reads the pack in it.\n\nWhen you export or publish, ModForge offers to translate by machine whatever is not yet in every language it has. Only what is still untranslated is sent, and a line you corrected yourself stays as you wrote it. To read or correct a translation, pick its language under Editing in, at the right end of the tabs, and edit as usual — it is saved with the pack.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstSteps.numberBesidePack.title = The number beside your pack

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstSteps.numberBesidePack.body = Publishing also moves the pack's version, and only publishing does. Saving does not, and neither does exporting: a version says what players were given, and an afternoon of saving gives them nothing.\n\nIt moves on its own, by what actually changed since your last release. Something new — a character, a room, a conversation — moves the middle number. A change to something that was already there moves the last one. The first is yours alone: type it into the Version box when you decide a release is a new thing rather than more of the old one.\n\nA place of your own is next: it builds a room and puts a door to it in the bedroom you wake up in, and every tutorial after that goes through the same door.

[Tutorial: A place of your own]

# Tutorials: a tutorial's name
tutorial.aPlace.title = A place of your own

# Tutorials: the line under a tutorial's name
tutorial.aPlace.summary = Build a room out of two layers, give it depth, and a door from your bedroom.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aPlace.roomsTwoPictures.title = Rooms are two pictures

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aPlace.roomsTwoPictures.body = A place is built from a base layer — the room itself — and a secondary layer seen past it. Keeping them apart is what lets a room have depth instead of looking like a painted backdrop.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aPlace.addPlace.title = Add a place

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aPlace.addPlace.body = Use + Place, under Your places. The other list is for adding things to rooms the game already has, which is a different job.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aPlace.addPlace.hint = + Place is in the toolbar above the left-hand list.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aPlace.roomItself.title = The room itself

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aPlace.roomItself.body = Set the back sprite to TutorialArt/Locations/RoomB.png. That is the layer players read as the room they are standing in.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aPlace.roomItself.hint = Level art, then the Back sprite row.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aPlace.somethingStanding.title = And something standing in it

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aPlace.somethingStanding.body = Now set the front sprite to TutorialArt/Locations/Room.png — a vase on a transparent background. This layer draws in front of the back one, so it reads as an object in the room rather than part of the wall behind it.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aPlace.somethingStanding.hint = Level art, then the Front sprite row.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aPlace.giveSomeDepth.title = Give it some depth

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aPlace.giveSomeDepth.body = Turn off Same as front under Behaviour, and set the back layer to drift by a different amount from the front. Anything different will do — the further apart they are, the deeper the room reads. Watch the preview with Preview parallax switched on.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aPlace.giveSomeDepth.hint = Untick Same as front, then change the Parallax — back value.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aPlace.wayBackOut.title = A way back out

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aPlace.wayBackOut.body = Add a navigator button and set its Target to My Room. These are the buttons along the bottom of a room, and without one your place is somewhere players can arrive but not leave. Sending it back to the bedroom closes the loop: in through the door you are about to build, out through this.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aPlace.wayBackOut.hint = + Add navigator button, then pick My Room in the row's Target box.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aPlace.sayButtonReads.title = Say what the button reads

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aPlace.sayButtonReads.body = Set the button's Label. Without one the game falls back to a name worked out from the target, which is usually close but rarely what you would have written — and it is the difference between a button that says "My Room" and one that says whatever the level happens to be called underneath.\n\nWrite what a player would call the place. The label also takes [PV:name] tokens, so a button can change what it says once something in the pack has happened.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aPlace.sayButtonReads.hint = The Label box on the navigator button row.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aPlace.doorBedroom.title = A door from your bedroom

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aPlace.doorBedroom.body = Now the way in. The bedroom every save starts in is the one place you can always reach, so that is where the door goes — a vanilla extension puts your own buttons onto a vanilla place's strip. Under Vanilla extensions, below the places list, use + Source.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aPlace.doorBedroom.hint = + Source is under Vanilla extensions, below the places list.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aPlace.aimBedroom.title = Aim it at the bedroom

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aPlace.aimBedroom.body = It arrives pointing at the Beach, which is only a default and not what you want. Set Source place to My Room — that is 5_MyRoom, the one you wake up in. An extension attaches your buttons to whichever vanilla place this names, so this box decides where the door is.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aPlace.aimBedroom.hint = Source place, in the pane on the right. Pick My Room.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aPlace.pointRoom.title = Point it at your room

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aPlace.pointRoom.body = Add a navigator button on that extension, and set its Target to the place you built. This is the whole trick for seeing your work: the bedroom is there the moment a save loads, so one button there reaches anything you build from now on without playing through to find it.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aPlace.pointRoom.hint = + Add navigator button, then set its Target to your place.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aPlace.goLook.title = Go and look at it

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aPlace.goLook.body = Save, export, and load any game. The button is on the bedroom's strip, and it takes you straight into the room you just built. Everything the later tutorials add goes in that room, so this one door is all you need from here.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aPlace.roomExists.title = A room that exists

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aPlace.roomExists.body = Two layers, some depth, and a round trip to your bedroom and back. Export and you can walk into it. Validate first — it will tell you if either button is pointing at nothing.

[Tutorial: Putting things in the room]

# Tutorials: a tutorial's name
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.title = Putting things in the room

# Tutorials: the line under a tutorial's name
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.summary = Add objects to a place, layer them, and leave one switched off.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.roomMoreThanTwo.title = A room is more than two pictures

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.roomMoreThanTwo.body = The back and front layers are the room itself. Everything ELSE in it is a GameObject: its own picture, at its own position, with its own place in the stack — and, unlike the layers, something a dialogue or a rule can switch on and off while the game runs.\n\nThat is how a room changes. A door that opens is two objects, one switched off. A coat left on a chair is an object that appears once a conversation has happened. A lamp that comes on at night is the same trick again.\n\nObject art is NOT a level layer, so it is not 2048 by 1136. Draw it at whatever size the thing really is, on a transparent background; it is placed in the room rather than filling it.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.openRoomBuilt.title = Open the room you built

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.openRoomBuilt.body = This carries on in the same pack. Click your place in the list on the left — the one you made two tutorials ago, not a vanilla extension.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.openRoomBuilt.hint = Your places are the upper list; vanilla extensions are below it.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.addObject.title = Add an object

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.addObject.body = Use + Add GameObject. It arrives empty and unnamed, which is fine — the next step is what makes it a thing.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.addObject.hint = + Add GameObject, under the GameObjects box.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.giveNamePicture.title = Give it a name and a picture

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.giveNamePicture.body = The name is how everything else finds this object — a rule that switches it on names it, and names repeat all over a level, so pick something you would recognise months from now.\n\nFor the picture use TutorialArt/Locations/Vase.png. It is worth looking at before you pick it: it is a vase and almost nothing else, on a transparent background, at the size a vase actually is. Compare that to Room.png, which contains the same vase inside a full 2048 by 1136 room — the padding IS the difference between a layer and a prop. Art with a room's worth of empty space around it cannot be placed, only laid over everything.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.giveNamePicture.hint = Select the object in the tree, then fill in Name and its sprite.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.putWhereWant.title = Put it where you want it

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.putWhereWant.body = Drag the object in the preview. The gizmo moves the real position — this is not a preview-only control, it is the same X and Y you could type in by hand, and typing is the fallback when you want an exact number rather than a look.\n\nPut the vase somewhere it could plausibly stand. It will be drawing over the whole room at the moment, which the next step fixes.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.putWhereWant.hint = Click the object in the preview, then drag it.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.decideStandsFront.title = Decide what it stands in front of

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.decideStandsFront.body = Sorting order is the stack. The back layer sits at -12 and the front layer at -10, so an object at -11 stands BETWEEN them: behind the furniture in the front layer, in front of the wall behind it. Anything above -10 draws over the whole room.\n\nA new object starts at 0, which is over everything. Set it to -11 and watch the preview put it into the room rather than on top of it.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.decideStandsFront.hint = Sorting order, on the selected object. -11 puts it between the layers.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.leaveSwitchedOff.title = Leave it switched off

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.leaveSwitchedOff.body = Untick Start active. The object is still in the room, at the position you gave it — it simply is not shown when the player arrives.\n\nThis is half of every change a room ever makes. The other half is a Set-Active action, from a dialogue node or an integration rule, naming this object. Build the object switched off now and the rule that reveals it has something to point at later.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.leaveSwitchedOff.hint = Start active, on the selected object. Untick it.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.previewTelling.title = What the preview is telling you

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.previewTelling.body = An object that starts switched off is drawn faded, so you can still position it — the game will draw nothing there until something turns it on. Fade objects that start inactive turns that off while you work.\n\nThe same goes for anything under a switched-off parent: the game draws none of it, so the preview fades all of it. If a whole group of your objects has gone pale, that is why.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.roomCanChange.title = A room that can change

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.dressingTheRoom.roomCanChange.body = You now have a room with something in it that is not part of the picture — placed, layered, and waiting to be switched on.\n\nObjects nest, so one can be a container for several and switching the parent moves all of them at once. And the Documentation section covers the rest of what a place carries: masks, weather, and the beach ambience a room can keep or drop.

[Tutorial: On the world map]

# Tutorials: a tutorial's name
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.title = On the world map

# Tutorials: the line under a tutorial's name
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.summary = Put a place on the world map so a player can travel to it directly.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.twoWaysReachRoom.title = Two ways to reach a room

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.twoWaysReachRoom.body = You have already built one: a navigator button on the bedroom's strip, which is a door from one room to another. It works, and it is the right shape for somewhere that sits BEHIND another place — a back office off a shop, a bedroom off a hall.\n\nThe world map is the other way, and it means something different. A place on the map is a destination in its own right rather than a room reached through another one.\n\nThe map is not open from everywhere, mind: the player reaches it by going to the Garage, into the Car, and pressing Drive. So a map button suits somewhere they would deliberately travel to, and a navigator button suits somewhere they walk into from next door. Most places want one or the other, not both.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.addMapButton.title = Add a map button

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.addMapButton.body = Use + Map button. It appears as a card in the list — the map buttons tab has no separate editor, each button IS its row.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.addMapButton.hint = + Map button, at the top of the tab.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.sayWhereGoes.title = Say where it goes

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.sayWhereGoes.body = Set Target place to the room you built. A button with no target is the quietest mistake in the editor: it saves, it exports, it appears on the map, and clicking it does nothing at all.\n\nValidate catches it, which is the reason to run Validate before every export rather than after something goes wrong.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.sayWhereGoes.hint = Target place, on the card you just added.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.chooseWhichMenuLives.title = Choose which menu it lives in

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.chooseWhichMenuLives.body = The world map is not one list of buttons — it is five radial menus, one per district: Seaside, The Line, Neon Row, Shopside and Foundry. District decides which one your button joins.\n\nA new button arrives in Foundry because it had to arrive somewhere. That is a default, not a decision. Pick the district your place actually belongs to — a player who opens the wrong menu will not find it, and nothing will tell them it exists.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.chooseWhichMenuLives.hint = District, on the same card. Any of the five except the one it started in.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.giveSomethingRead.title = Give it something to read

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.giveSomethingRead.body = Label is the text on the button. Write what a player would call the place, not what you called it in the editor.\n\nIt also accepts [PV:name], which is replaced with one of your pack's variables while the game runs — so a button can read "The Shop" before a conversation and "Anna's Shop" after one, without a second button.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.giveSomethingRead.hint = Label, on the same card.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.twoCanLeaveAlone.title = The two you can leave alone

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.twoCanLeaveAlone.body = Music switches the background track when the button is used, which is worth setting for somewhere with its own atmosphere and worth leaving empty otherwise — an empty Music keeps whatever was playing.\n\nConditions decide whether the button is shown at all. An empty list means always, which is what you want for now. Fill it in and the place stays off the map until the player has earned it — the same condition rows you will meet on dialogues and rules.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.somewhereGo.title = Somewhere to go

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.onTheWorldMap.somewhereGo.body = Validate, export, and open the world map in game: your place is in the district you chose, under the label you wrote.\n\nYou now have both routes — a door from the bedroom and a place on the map — and they are independent. Removing one does not affect the other, which is worth knowing when a place becomes reachable two ways by accident.

[Tutorial: Your first conversation]

# Tutorials: a tutorial's name
tutorial.firstConversation.title = Your first conversation

# Tutorials: the line under a tutorial's name
tutorial.firstConversation.summary = Write a dialogue that starts in your room, with a choice in it.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstConversation.dialogueTree.title = A dialogue is a tree

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstConversation.dialogueTree.body = Play runs down the node list from the top, and children are just nodes nested under a parent — reaching the end of a branch carries on to whatever is next in the list, not back out. Every node is one of three kinds. Text plays its line and moves on. Choice stops and offers its children to the player. Random picks one child itself. Everything else on a node — who speaks, what happens after, what it sets — hangs off that.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstConversation.addDialogue.title = Add a dialogue

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstConversation.addDialogue.body = Use + Dialogue. It needs a place to live in, which is why the room came first.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.firstConversation.addDialogue.hint = + Dialogue is in the toolbar above the list.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstConversation.sayWhereHappens.title = Say where it happens

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstConversation.sayWhereHappens.body = Set the level in the start conditions to the place you built. A dialogue with no level will never start, because nothing tells the game where to look for it.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.firstConversation.sayWhereHappens.hint = Start conditions, then the level row pinned at the top.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstConversation.writeLine.title = Write a line

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstConversation.writeLine.body = Add a root node, choose your character as the actor, and give them something to say. One line is a conversation.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.firstConversation.writeLine.hint = + Root, then fill in Actor and Text in the Node box.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstConversation.restNodeBox.title = The rest of the Node box

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstConversation.restNodeBox.body = Actor is who speaks, and leaving it empty plays the line with whoever is already on screen. Expression and Outfit swap the bust mid-conversation — your practice character has neither, so leave them be. Tag names a node so a jump can find it. Duration decides whether the line waits for a click or times out on its own.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstConversation.givePlayerSay.title = Give the player a say

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstConversation.givePlayerSay.body = Add a second node, set its Kind to Choice, and hang two children off it with + Child. The Choice node's own Text is the prompt, shown under the options — it is what the player is answering. Each child IS an option, and the child's Text is the label on its button, not a line somebody says. Write all three: anything left blank shows up in game as blank, which is what an empty node looks like from the outside.\n\nGive the CHOICE node an actor too — its text is spoken, so it needs somebody to speak it. The children do not: an option is a button, not a line, and nobody says it aloud.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.firstConversation.givePlayerSay.hint = Set Kind to Choice, write its Text, + Child twice, then write each child's Text.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstConversation.happensAfterNode.title = What happens after a node

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstConversation.happensAfterNode.body = Jump decides where play goes next. Continue — the default — moves to the next node down the list, whether that is a child of this one or something further out. Exit stops the conversation and skips everything below. Jump resumes at whichever node carries the Tag you name.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstConversation.makeExitMeanSomething.title = Make Exit mean something

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstConversation.makeExitMeanSomething.body = At the very bottom of the list Continue and Exit are the same thing, because there is nothing left to skip either way. So give Exit something to skip: add one more root node below the choice with a closing line, then set ONE of your two options to Exit. Take that option in game and the conversation stops; take the other and it carries on into the closing line.\n\nGive the closing line an actor as well. Every node that SAYS something needs one — a line with no speaker shows no name, which reads as a fault rather than as a narrator.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.firstConversation.makeExitMeanSomething.hint = + Root for the closing line, then select an option and set Jump to Exit.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstConversation.actionsLineChangesThings.title = Actions are how a line changes things

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstConversation.actionsLineChangesThings.body = Actions on start and on finish hang off any node, and they are what makes a conversation matter: set a variable, unlock a wallpaper, swap a sprite, play a sound. Later tutorials use them properly. Worth knowing now that the line is the visible half and the actions are the other one.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstConversation.checkBeforeExport.title = Check it before you export

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstConversation.checkBeforeExport.body = Validate catches what the editor can see: an option with no text, an actor the pack never defines, a jump aimed at a tag no node carries, a level renamed underneath the dialogue. Run it now — a dialogue is the easiest thing in a pack to leave half-finished, because a blank node looks the same as a filled one in the tree.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.firstConversation.goHaveConversation.title = Go and have the conversation

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.firstConversation.goHaveConversation.body = Export, load a save, and take the bedroom button through to your room. The dialogue starts on arrival, because that is what the level in its start conditions means. Reading your own line in the game tells you things the editor cannot — whether it is too long, and whether the choice actually reads as a choice.

[Tutorial: Lines that choose themselves]

# Tutorials: a tutorial's name
tutorial.linesThatChoose.title = Lines that choose themselves

# Tutorials: the line under a tutorial's name
tutorial.linesThatChoose.summary = Gate a line on the state of the room, and let two speakers share a scene.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.linesThatChoose.notEveryLineShould.title = Not every line should play

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.linesThatChoose.notEveryLineShould.body = So far every node in your dialogue runs when play reaches it. A conversation that reacts to anything needs the opposite: lines that sometimes do not.\n\nThat is what a node's own Conditions box is for, and it behaves in two different ways depending on where the node sits. An ordinary node whose conditions fail is SKIPPED — play carries on to the next one. A node directly under a Choice is not offered at all: the player never sees that option, rather than seeing it greyed out.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.linesThatChoose.openConversationWrote.title = Open the conversation you wrote

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.linesThatChoose.openConversationWrote.body = Same pack, same dialogue. Pick it in the list, then select any node in the tree — the one you want the condition on.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.linesThatChoose.openConversationWrote.hint = Click the dialogue, then a node in the list beside it.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.linesThatChoose.gateSomethingReal.title = Gate it on something real

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.linesThatChoose.gateSomethingReal.body = Add a condition to that node with + Add condition, then finish it — an empty condition row is not a gate, it is a blank that always passes.\n\nSet its Type to GameObjectActive. The row then asks the same three things in the same order the Set-Active action asks them: Category, Level, Target. That is deliberate — a condition reads back exactly what that action sets, so it addresses the object the same way.\n\nSet Category to GameObjects, since the object lives inside a place. Set Level to your place: the Target dropdown stays disabled until you do, because it lists that level's objects and nothing else. Then pick the object you put in the room and left switched off.\n\nDoing this against something you already built matters. The object is genuinely off, so the line genuinely will not play — and when a rule switches the object on later, the line starts appearing on its own. The conversation is reading the state of the room.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.linesThatChoose.gateSomethingReal.hint = Type = GameObjectActive, then Category, Level and Target below it.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.linesThatChoose.conditionsDoNotStop.title = Conditions do not stop at objects

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.linesThatChoose.conditionsDoNotStop.body = The same box offers everything the pack can ask about: which place is on screen, what a variable holds, whether it is raining, a percentage rolled once a day. Every condition in the list has to pass, and an empty list always passes — which is why a node with no conditions always plays.\n\nAny condition can also be negated, which is usually shorter than writing the opposite one.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.linesThatChoose.someoneElseRoom.title = Someone else in the room

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.linesThatChoose.someoneElseRoom.body = A conversation is rarely one voice. The Actor field on a node says who speaks it, and it holds from that node onward until another one changes it — you do not set it per line.\n\nEvery pack has a speaker it did not create: the player. Add a node and set its Actor to the player, and the line is the player talking back. Any mod addressing you reaches that same character, which is why its name and colour are fixed and yours to use rather than define.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.linesThatChoose.someoneElseRoom.hint = Add a node, write a line, then set Actor to player.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.linesThatChoose.lettingOneOptionSay.title = Letting one option say more

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.linesThatChoose.lettingOneOptionSay.body = Last tutorial you gave one option Exit and let the other fall through to your closing line. Both are still one line long. Real dialogue is lopsided — one answer earns a reaction, the other does not — and the branch that reacts still has to end up somewhere, or you write the ending twice.\n\nA TAG is a name you give a node so another node can send play to it. Two fields do it. Tag, on the node being aimed AT: a short word, yours to choose, and it is a label rather than anything the player sees. Jump, on the node doing the aiming: set it to Jump, and a Jump to box appears listing the tags in this dialogue.\n\nBuild it in that order — the tag first, so it is there to be picked. Tag your closing line, the one you added last tutorial, with something like ending. Then take the option you did NOT set to Exit, add a Child under it, and write the reaction that answer deserves. On that child set Jump to Jump, and choose ending in the Jump to box.\n\nPlay now runs the option, the reaction, and then lands on the closing line. The other option still ends where it ended. One ending, written once, reached two ways — and nothing repeats, because every jump here goes forward.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.linesThatChoose.lettingOneOptionSay.hint = Tag the closing line, then + Child on the non-Exit option and set its Jump to that tag.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.linesThatChoose.jumpingBackwardsPurpose.title = Jumping backwards on purpose

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.linesThatChoose.jumpingBackwardsPurpose.body = Forward is not the only direction, and backwards is not a mistake. Jumping back to a Choice is one of the most useful shapes there is, and the game itself is full of it: a menu where each option answers and then returns, except one that carries the conversation on. Ask about the weather, come back. Ask about her sister, come back. Say you should get going, and that one continues.\n\nTo build it, tag the Choice node itself, then give the last line of each returning branch a jump to that tag. The option that leaves simply does not jump — it continues, or exits.\n\nThe one thing to be sure of is a way out. If every option comes back, the player cannot leave; at least one has to not return. Put that option in the menu from the start rather than behind a condition, or a player who fails the condition is stuck in a conversation with no exit.\n\nAnd give a tag to exactly one node. Two nodes carrying the same tag make every jump aimed at it ambiguous: play goes to whichever the game reaches first, which is not something you chose and may not stay the same.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.linesThatChoose.conversationMemoryRoom.title = A conversation with a memory of the room

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.linesThatChoose.conversationMemoryRoom.body = Your dialogue now has a line that only plays when the room is in a particular state, a second speaker, and a branch that rejoins.\n\nWhat it still cannot do is remember anything across visits — the room's state is the room's, not the conversation's. That is what variables are for, and they are the next group.

[Tutorial: A face that moves]

# Tutorials: a tutorial's name
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.title = A face that moves

# Tutorials: the line under a tutorial's name
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.summary = Give a bust its blink, its mouth and its expressions.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.bustStackPictures.title = A bust is a stack of pictures

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.bustStackPictures.body = The base sprite is the whole character. Everything else is a small picture laid over the same 256 by 256 frame, in the same place, swapped in when the game wants it.\n\nThere are three kinds. A BLINK frame is the eyes closed, shown for a fifth of a second every few seconds. Four MOUTH frames cycle while a line types, so the character looks like they are speaking. Four EXPRESSIONS — Happy, Angry, Sad and Flirty — are swapped in by a dialogue line and stay until something changes them.\n\nEach is drawn on a transparent background at the same size as the base, so it lines up. Draw only the part that changes: for a blink, that is a pair of closed eyes and nothing else.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.openCharacterMade.title = Open the character you made

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.openCharacterMade.body = This carries on in the same pack. Overlays belong to an OUTFIT, not to the character, because a character in a different outfit blinks with different eyes. Click your character in the tree, then the outfit underneath it.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.openCharacterMade.hint = Expand the character to see its outfits, and click one.

Bust1's is Bust1Blink.png.\n\nRemember what the tickbox means: ticked with nothing behind it is a broken outfit, and the character will not appear at all.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while

# Tutorials: the heading of a step

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.watchHappen.body = Look at the preview — it is already blinking. The eyes shut every few seconds on a random wait, exactly as the game runs it, so blink art that is off by a few pixels shows itself here rather than in game.\n\nBlinking is the tickbox under the preview, on by default. Untick it to hold the eyes open while you look at something else. It is a preview control and changes nothing in the pack.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.fourMouthsNamedPrefix.title = Four mouths, named by a prefix

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.fourMouthsNamedPrefix.body = Mouth frames are not chosen one by one. You give a PREFIX — a path from the pack root, like the Base and Blink rows above, but stopping short of the number and the .png. The game adds 1, 2, 3 and 4 to it, so TutorialArt/Busts/Bust1/Mouth finds Mouth1.png through Mouth4.png. The grey text after the box shows what it is currently going to look for.\n\nThat is exactly four frames: not three, not five.\n\nTick Has Mouth frames. The folder is already filled in — it was copied from the blink path you just set, since overlays for one outfit live together — so what is left is the filename part. Put Mouth on the end of it.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.fourMouthsNamedPrefix.hint = Has Mouth frames, then add Mouth after the folder already in the box.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.hearTalk.title = Hear it talk

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.hearTalk.body = Tick Yapping under the preview — unlike Blinking this one starts off — and the mouth cycles the way it does while a line types.\n\nMouth frame beside it holds one frame still instead, which is how you check a single drawing: pick 1 to 4 to see that frame on its own, or Closed for none of them.\n\nBoth are preview controls. Neither changes the pack.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.fourExpressionsNamedSame.title = Four expressions, named the same way

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.fourExpressionsNamedSame.body = Expressions work like mouths, except the game appends a NAME rather than a number — and only four names exist: Happy, Angry, Sad and Flirty. A prefix of TutorialArt/Busts/Bust1/Expression finds ExpressionHappy.png and its three siblings.\n\nSame box, at the bottom: tick Has Expressions, and again the folder is already there. Add Expression after it.\n\nAny other name is never looked for. If you want a fifth mood, it has to be a different outfit.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.fourExpressionsNamedSame.hint = Has Expressions, at the bottom of the Sprites and expressions box.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.tryThem.title = Try them on

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aFaceThatMoves.tryThem.body = The Expression dropdown under the preview swaps between them — try each one and watch the face change, with None for the plain base.\n\nIn a conversation this is a node's Expression field, and it holds until another node changes it: an angry line does not go back to neutral by itself.\n\nThat is the whole bust: one base picture, one blink, four mouths, four expressions. Everything a character does on screen comes out of those, and you now have all of them.

[Tutorial: Making it move]

# Tutorials: a tutorial's name
tutorial.makingItMove.title = Making it move

# Tutorials: the line under a tutorial's name
tutorial.makingItMove.summary = Paint a jiggle mask and tune it until the bust moves the way you want.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.makingItMove.maskNotArtwork.title = A mask is not artwork

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.makingItMove.maskNotArtwork.body = It records how much of an effect each part of a sprite gets, and it holds three separate layers. Bounce moves those pixels up and down. Wave slides them side to side, in a wave that travels up the sprite. Noise scatters them irregularly. A fourth value, the overall intensity, scales all three at once — so black is no movement, and brighter is more. Painting a mask is deciding where a bust moves, which way, and by how much.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.makingItMove.openCharacterMade.title = Open the character you made

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.makingItMove.openCharacterMade.body = This carries on in the same pack, on the same character as the first tutorial — masks belong to an outfit, so there has to be one selected before anything here applies to it. Click your character in the tree, then the outfit underneath it.

# Tutorials: what the step says

Point this outfit's base sprite at one of the fuller ones instead — the effect is far easier to judge when there is more of it to see.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.makingItMove.pickBustSomethingMove.hint = TutorialArt/Busts/Bust4 or Bust5 give you the most to work with.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.makingItMove.paintWhereShouldMove.title = Paint where it should move

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.makingItMove.paintWhereShouldMove.body = Open Edit Mask and paint the Bounce layer over the parts that should move. Keep away from the edges — a mask that reaches the outline drags the whole silhouette and reads as a wobble rather than weight.\n\nSaving offers to put the file beside the bust art, named after it: Bust4Base.png gets Bust4BaseMask.png. Nothing requires that — a mask can live anywhere inside the pack — but a mask is unreadable on its own, and months later the only thing that says which sprite it was drawn for is where it sits and what it is called.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.makingItMove.paintWhereShouldMove.hint = Edit Mask, brush with B, paint, then Save (Ctrl+S) in that window.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.makingItMove.jiggleNumbersDo.title = What the Jiggle numbers do

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.makingItMove.jiggleNumbersDo.body = The mask says WHERE the sprite moves; these say how. Strength is how far the pixels travel and Speed how fast the cycle runs. Wave frequency is how many waves fit across the sprite, so a low number sways and a high one ripples. The three Noise settings keep it from ticking like a metronome — Scale is how fine the grain is, Speed how fast it drifts, Strength how much of it shows through. Tint multiplies the sprite's colour and is almost always left white. The defaults are a sensible bust: change one at a time and watch the preview, because the numbers mean far less than what you see.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.makingItMove.dropletsOverHereToo.title = The droplets, over here too

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.makingItMove.dropletsOverHereToo.body = Under Jiggle there is a Particles box with one tickbox in it. The game's Wet particle preset — the droplets it uses for swim and shower art — is attached to every pack bust whether you ask for it or not; the tickbox only decides whether it is PLAYING when the bust appears.\n\nLeave it off for an ordinary outfit, or a dry character talks to the player in the rain. Tick it on the swimsuit or just-out-of-the-shower variant, which is the whole reason it exists.\n\nNPCs have the same setting on their own tab, with one difference worth knowing now: for an NPC the emitter's POSITION is set per placement rather than on the definition, because the same figure can stand in more than one room.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.makingItMove.previewCanShow.title = What the preview can show you

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.makingItMove.previewCanShow.body = Breathing is already on, and it is the mask doing its work — the fastest way to tell whether you painted too much. Depth beside it is how pronounced that idle motion is.\n\nThe other controls drive the overlays you added last tutorial: Blinking cycles the blink frame, Yapping runs the mouth, Mouth frame holds one frame still so you can look at it, and Expression swaps in one of the four faces. Turn them on while the mask is running — that combination is what the player actually sees.\n\nWatch the face while you do, because the mask does not reach it. Only the base sprite is displaced; the blink, the mouth and the expressions sit on top of it exactly where they were drawn and do not move at all.\n\nSo paint across the face and the face slides out from under its own eyes and mouth. Nothing looks wrong until an overlay is showing, which is why it is easy to miss: turn Blinking and Yapping on and the drift is obvious.\n\nThat is the whole reason most busts move at the chest and the hair and leave the face alone. Keep the mask off it, or dark enough there that nothing separates.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.makingItMove.thenJudgeGame.title = Then judge it in the game

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.makingItMove.thenJudgeGame.body = Export and go through to your room, so the character speaks with the mask running. The preview and the game use the same code and the same numbers, but a bust at the size players see it, mid-line, is where too much movement gives itself away.

[Tutorial: Populating the room]

# Tutorials: a tutorial's name
tutorial.populating.title = Populating the room

tutorial.populating.summary = Add standing figures to your place, with shadows that sit them on the floor.

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.populating.npcsSceneryNotSpeakers.body = They stand in rooms and make them look lived in. Anyone who talks is a character instead — the two are separate on purpose, because most figures in a room never say anything.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.populating.defineOne.title = Define one
