# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.populating.dropletsDoNotWant.title = The droplets you do not want

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.populating.dropletsDoNotWant.body = Wet particles are the droplet effect the game uses for swim and shower variants. They are off for a new NPC, which is what you want here — on a dry figure standing in a room they read as rain indoors. Tick Enabled when you do want them, and the emitter's position is then set per placement rather than here, because the same NPC can stand in more than one room.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.populating.sitThemFloor.title = Sit them on the floor

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.populating.sitThemFloor.body = The shadow under an NPC is already on, and it is the cheapest thing there is for how a room reads: without one a figure looks pasted onto the picture rather than standing in it. Colour sets how dark it is, and Sorting order how far forward it draws — well under the figure, so it never crosses a leg. Worth turning OFF only for someone who is not standing on anything, like a face at a window.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.populating.sitThemFloor.hint = Shadow, then tick Enabled.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.populating.giveThemSomeLife.title = Give them some life

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.populating.giveThemSomeLife.body = Paint a jiggle mask for the NPC, the same way you did for a bust. A standing figure with no mask is completely still, which reads as a cardboard cut-out the moment anything else on screen moves.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.populating.giveThemSomeLife.hint = Edit Mask, beside the Jiggle mask field.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.populating.putThemRoom.title = Put them in the room

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.populating.putThemRoom.body = Back on Places, use + Add NPC on the place you built. That gives you an empty placement row, and the first field on it is the one that matters: NPC. Pick the one you just defined.\n\nA row with that field blank is a placement of nobody. It costs nothing, breaks nothing and shows nothing, which is exactly why it is easy to leave behind and hard to find later.\n\nThe same NPC can stand in as many rooms as you like — you are placing a copy, not moving the original.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.populating.putThemRoom.hint = + Add NPC under GameObjects, then pick your NPC in the row's NPC box.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.populating.switchThem.title = Switch them on

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.populating.switchThem.body = A placement starts INACTIVE. The default assumes the common pattern — a parked variant that some rule switches on later — and yours has nothing to switch it on. Tick Start active on the placement, or the room will be empty when you walk into it and nothing will say why.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.populating.switchThem.hint = Start active, on the placement's own row under GameObjects.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.populating.placeThemProperly.title = Place them properly

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.populating.placeThemProperly.body = Drag them somewhere that makes sense for the room, and check the sorting order so they are not standing behind the furniture. The preview shows it at the size players will see.\n\nClick the figure and a small toolbar appears in the top-right corner of the preview. Its first row is what you are moving: Body, Shadow, Blink or Wet. The second is how — Move, Rotate or Scale.\n\nThat is how a shadow gets nudged out from under someone standing half behind a table, or the droplet emitter gets lifted to where the water would actually be. All four live on the PLACEMENT, not on the NPC, so moving the shadow here does not move it in any other room the same NPC stands in.\n\nThe same fields are on the placement row under Part transforms, if you would rather type a number than drag.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.populating.seeWhetherRoomReads.title = See whether the room reads

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.populating.seeWhetherRoomReads.body = Export and walk in through the bedroom button. A room with people in it is the first time a place stops looking like artwork, and standing in it is the only way to tell whether they are in the way or in the right place.

[Tutorial: One person, two places]

# Tutorials: a tutorial's name
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.title = One person, two places

# Tutorials: the line under a tutorial's name
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.summary = Reuse an NPC, and give it a reflection and a blink so it sits in the room.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.definitionNotAppearance.title = A definition is not an appearance

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.definitionNotAppearance.body = The NPCs tab does not put anybody in a room. It DEFINES one: the art, the movement, the blink, the shadow. Putting them somewhere is a separate act, on the Places tab, and it is called a placement.\n\nOne definition can have any number of placements — the same figure in three rooms, or twice in one. Change the definition and every placement changes with it. Change a placement and only that one moves.\n\nThat split is the whole point of the tab. It is also the thing to get straight before you wonder why editing one NPC moved another.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.putSamePersonTwice.title = Put the same person in twice

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.putSamePersonTwice.body = Go to your room and add the NPC you already made a SECOND time. Two placements, one definition — the same drawing standing in two spots.\n\nIn a real pack this is a crowd: one figure, placed six times at different positions and scales, is six people as far as the player is concerned.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.putSamePersonTwice.hint = + Add NPC on your place, then pick the NPC you defined.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.tellThemApart.title = Tell them apart

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.tellThemApart.body = Give the new placement its own Name. This is the placement's name, not the NPC's: it is what a Set-Active action points at when a rule wants to hide THIS one and leave the other standing.\n\nTwo placements with the same name in one room is the mistake to avoid — a rule aiming at that name reaches whichever the game finds first, and which one that is may change.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.tellThemApart.hint = Select the new placement and type a Name unlike the other one's.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.backDefinition.title = Back to the definition

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.backDefinition.body = Now change something on the NPC itself and watch BOTH placements follow. Open the NPCs tab and select the NPC you defined — the next two steps act on it.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.standThemSomething.title = Stand them on something

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.standThemSomething.body = Turn on Reflection. It draws a mirrored copy beneath the pose, and it is what makes a figure look like it is standing on a wet street or a polished floor rather than in front of one.\n\nIt is off by default because most floors are not reflective, and a reflection on a carpet reads as a mistake. Alpha is how visible it is — the game's own reflections are a faint wash, not a second character — and Y offset moves it to meet the feet rather than the middle of the sprite.\n\nYou do not have to guess that offset. Once reflection is on, a Reflection chip appears in the preview toolbar on the Places tab alongside Body, Shadow, Blink and Wet — pick it and drag the reflection up or down against the actual floor of the actual room.\n\nIt drags vertically and nothing else, because that offset is the only number there is: the mirror is dropped a full pose height automatically, and this is the correction on top. And unlike the other four, it belongs to the NPC rather than the placement, so moving it here moves it in every room that NPC stands in. The toolbar says so while it is selected.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.standThemSomething.hint = Enabled, in the Reflection box.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.giveThemEyesClose.title = Give them eyes that close

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.giveThemEyesClose.body = An NPC blinks the same way a bust does, from one eyes-closed frame drawn over the pose. Point Blink sprite at the blink art beside the pose you used: TutorialArt/NPCs/Dummy, where DummyNPC0.png has DummyNPC0Blink.png next to it.\n\nOpen wait min and max are the range the gap between blinks is drawn from, so a room full of the same figure does not blink in unison, and Closed hold is how long each blink lasts. Leave a blank blink sprite and the NPC simply does not blink, which is a fine answer.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.giveThemEyesClose.hint = Blink sprite, in the Blink box.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.oneLeaveAlone.title = The one to leave alone

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.oneLeaveAlone.body = Wet particles are the droplet effect, and they are off by default for a reason: they suit somebody who has just come out of the water and look like a fault on anybody else. Enabled turns the effect on at all; Start active decides whether it is already running when the room loads, rather than waiting for something to start it.\n\nLeave both off. Knowing where they are is enough.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.roomPeople.title = A room with people in it

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.npcsThatBelong.roomPeople.body = Both placements now reflect and blink, because both are the same definition — and they stand in different spots under different names, because a placement is its own thing.\n\nThat is the shape to remember: art and behaviour on the NPC, position and identity on the placement. A crowd is one definition and many placements; a cast is many definitions.

[Tutorial: Sound in a room]

# Tutorials: a tutorial's name
tutorial.soundInARoom.title = Sound in a room

# Tutorials: the line under a tutorial's name
tutorial.soundInARoom.summary = Add a music track and a sound effect, and make one fire from a line.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.soundInARoom.twoDifferentJobs.title = Two different jobs

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.soundInARoom.twoDifferentJobs.body = Music is the track underneath everything: one at a time, looping, changed by an action or by a map button. SFX are one-shots — a door, a slap, a splash — and several can play at once without cutting each other off.\n\nBoth take OGG, WAV or MP3, so use whatever your source gives you rather than converting for the sake of it. The practice files are deliberately one of each: an MP3 track and a WAV effect.\n\nKeep music long enough to loop without becoming obvious, and effects short. An effect that runs for two seconds is still playing when the line that fired it has gone.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.soundInARoom.addTrack.title = Add a track

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.soundInARoom.addTrack.body = Use + Music, then point Audio path at TutorialArt/Audio/Neon Rain.mp3 — the longer of the two files in that folder, and the one meant to loop under a scene.\n\nGive it a display name you would recognise in a list of twenty. Note the Runtime name beside it: that key is what everything else in the pack asks for this track by, and you will type it into a button in two steps' time.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.soundInARoom.addTrack.hint = + Music, then Audio path in the pane on the right.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.soundInARoom.hearWithoutLeavingEditor.title = Hear it without leaving the editor

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.soundInARoom.hearWithoutLeavingEditor.body = Play sounds the track at the volume beside it. It plays ONCE here whatever Loop says — looping is a runtime behaviour, and the preview is for checking the file is the file you meant.\n\nLoop can be left blank, and blank means "do what the game normally does" rather than off. Volume starts at 1.0, which is the game's own level for a track. Change either only when you want to differ from that.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.soundInARoom.makeSomethingPlay.title = Make something play it

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.soundInARoom.makeSomethingPlay.body = A track nobody starts is a file in a folder. The shortest way to hear yours in game is the door you already built: back in tutorial 3 you put a navigator button on My Room pointing at your place.\n\nOpen that vanilla extension on the Places tab, find the button, and put your track's RUNTIME NAME in its Music box — the key from the Music tab, not the display name and not the filename. Walking through that door now changes the music.\n\nOne track plays at a time, and pressing the button switches to this one, so a button that leads back out wants a Music of its own or the room's track follows the player out. The same box lists the game's own tracks under their own heading, and the one called Music is the ordinary everyday music — that is usually what the way back out should say.\n\nMap buttons have the same box, and it is the more common place for it: the world map is how a player reaches most locations, so if you built one in tutorial 4, give it the same treatment. Same field, same runtime name, same one-at-a-time rule.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.soundInARoom.makeSomethingPlay.hint = Places tab, your My Room extension, the navigator button's Music box.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.soundInARoom.addEffect.title = Add an effect

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.soundInARoom.addEffect.body = Now the SFX tab. Same shape: + SFX, then Audio path at the OTHER file in TutorialArt/Audio — the short WAV, which is a door opening. Its filename is somebody's credit for the recording, which is why it looks like that and why it has not been renamed.\n\nDefault volume is what plays when something asks for this effect without saying how loud — which is most of the time.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.soundInARoom.addEffect.hint = + SFX, then Audio path in the pane on the right.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.soundInARoom.partWouldNeverGuess.title = The part you would never guess

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.soundInARoom.partWouldNeverGuess.body = An effect does not need an action. Auto-trigger patterns is a comma-separated list of text, and whenever one of those appears in a line of dialogue the effect fires by itself.\n\nThe convention is a lowercase word between asterisks — *door* — because it reads as a stage direction in the script and never collides with an ordinary word. Put a pattern in now.\n\nThis is how a scene gets its sound without every line carrying an action. It is also completely invisible from the tab, which is the only reason this step exists.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.soundInARoom.partWouldNeverGuess.hint = Auto-trigger patterns, on the SFX you just made. Try *door*.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.soundInARoom.sayLine.title = Say it in a line

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.soundInARoom.sayLine.body = Go back to your conversation and put the pattern into a node's text — the actual characters, asterisks and all. When that line plays, the effect plays with it.\n\nThe pattern stays in the text as written, so pick something you are content for a player to read, or keep it to a line where a stage direction belongs.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.soundInARoom.sayLine.hint = Select a node and add your pattern to its Text.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.soundInARoom.otherWayFireOne.title = The other way to fire one

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.soundInARoom.otherWayFireOne.body = Patterns are not the only route. A node can carry a PlaySFX action, on start or on finish, naming the effect by its runtime name — with a Volume to override the default and a Delay to hold it back a moment.\n\nThe two do the same job and differ in exactly one way: the pattern has to be IN the line, so the player reads *door* along with everything else. The action does not, so the sound plays over a line with nothing odd in it. Everything else is the same, including the random variants in the next step.\n\nUse patterns where the stage direction belongs in the script, and the action where it does not. Add one now, on the same conversation.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.soundInARoom.otherWayFireOne.hint = + Add action on a node, Type = PlaySFX, then pick your effect.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.soundInARoom.moreThanOneTake.title = More than one take

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.soundInARoom.moreThanOneTake.body = One more thing with no field for it. Put extra recordings beside the first with _1, _2, _3 on the end of the same name, and the game picks between them at random each time the effect plays.\n\nAudio/Door.wav picks up Audio/Door_1.wav and Audio/Door_2.wav on its own. Number them without gaps — counting stops at the first missing number, so _1, _2, _4 loads two of the three. The extensions do not have to match, so a WAV alongside an OGG is fine.\n\nIt is the difference between a door that sounds the same every time and one that does not. Both routes draw from the same set: a pattern in a line and a PlaySFX action pick a variant the same way.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.soundInARoom.packMakesNoise.title = A pack that makes noise

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.soundInARoom.packMakesNoise.body = You have a track, an effect, and a line that fires it without being asked. Scenes and Wallpapers are the other two media tabs and you have already met both — a scene in the rules tutorial, a wallpaper in the variables one.\n\nThat is every tab in the editor. What is left is not more kinds of thing, it is more of what you can already do.

[Tutorial: Remembering things]

# Tutorials: a tutorial's name
tutorial.remembering.title = Remembering things

# Tutorials: the line under a tutorial's name
tutorial.remembering.summary = Give the pack a memory, and use it to gate what players can see.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.remembering.packsForgetDefault.title = Packs forget by default

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.remembering.packsForgetDefault.body = Every conversation starts the same way unless something remembers what happened. Variables are that memory, and almost everything interesting in a pack is built on them.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.remembering.declareOne.title = Declare one

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.remembering.declareOne.body = Press + Variable. It arrives called var1, with Type already set to Bool — a yes/no value, which is what you want. Change Name to something that says what it MEANS: Met_Anna reads far better in a condition six months from now than Flag_3 or var1 does. No spaces, and you will be picking this name from a list later, so make it one you will recognise.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.remembering.declareOne.hint = + Variable, then type over var1 in the Name box.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.remembering.persistedWhyAlready.title = Persisted, and why it is already on

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.remembering.persistedWhyAlready.body = Persisted is ticked for you, and it means the value is written to the pack's save file — so what the player did is still true next time they load. Leave it on here. You would untick it for something that only matters during one visit, like a flag saying "this conversation already greeted them", which should start fresh every session rather than being remembered forever.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.remembering.setConversation.title = Set it from a conversation

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.remembering.setConversation.body = Nothing has changed the variable yet — declaring one only says it exists. Four things, in order. One: on the Dialogues tab, click a node in the tree to select it. Two: under Actions on finish, press + Add action. Three: set that row's Type to Variable, and leave Operation on Set and Source on Pack. Four: choose your variable in the Variable box, and choose True — a Bool gets True and False buttons rather than a text field, so there is no spelling to get wrong. Actions on finish runs when the player moves past the line, so the value changes once they have read it.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.remembering.setConversation.hint = + Add action, Type = Variable, Operation = Set, pick your Variable, choose True.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.remembering.giveThemSomethingUnlock.title = Give them something to unlock

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.remembering.giveThemSomethingUnlock.body = Now something the variable can gate. Press + Wallpaper and set its Sprite to TutorialArt/Wallpapers/DummyWPP.png. Then press + Add condition and fill that row in: Type is Variable, Source is Pack, Variable is the one you named, and the value is True. That is the whole mechanism — one place writes the value, another asks what it says.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.remembering.giveThemSomethingUnlock.hint = + Wallpaper, set Sprite, then + Add condition with Type = Variable.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.remembering.whenOneValueNot.title = When one value is not enough

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.remembering.whenOneValueNot.body = Bool, Int, Float and String each hold ONE thing. A LIST holds any number of strings, in order, and it exists for the question the other four answer badly: which of these has the player already done?\n\nSix topics they can ask about is six Bools and six conditions with the other four. It is one List with the topics they have used in it, and the same single condition on every option.\n\nAdd one now: + Variable, Type set to List. Give it a name that reads as a plural, because that is what it holds — TopicsDiscussed rather than Topic.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.remembering.whenOneValueNot.hint = + Variable, then set Type to List and rename it.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.remembering.puttingThingsAskingThere.title = Putting things in and asking what is there

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.remembering.puttingThingsAskingThere.body = A list has its own verbs, and they are the whole feature.\n\nTo CHANGE it, from a node action: Add to list appends a value, Remove from list takes the first matching one back out, and Clear list empties it. Adding the same value twice really does store it twice — nothing de-duplicates for you, so check before you add if that matters.\n\nTo ASK about it, from conditions: ListContains passes when a particular value is in there — negate it for "has not been used yet", which is the shape you want on a menu option. ListCount compares how many entries there are against a number, so equals zero means empty and negating that means something is in it.\n\nTwo more read it: Random from list picks one entry and writes it into another variable, and List count writes the size into an Int. Both are operations on the Variable action rather than actions of their own.\n\nAdd one to your list from a node now — an action on finish, Add to list, naming the list and a value.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.remembering.puttingThingsAskingThere.hint = + Add action on a node, Type = AddToList, then the list and a value.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.remembering.listWillActuallyUse.title = The list you will actually use it for

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.remembering.listWillActuallyUse.body = The pattern worth remembering, because it turns up constantly: a Choice whose options each add their own name to a list and gate themselves on NOT containing it.\n\nThe player sees the topics they have not asked about yet, the menu shrinks as they work through it, and when the last one goes the choice is empty — so keep one option outside the pattern that always shows and ends the conversation.\n\nWritten with Bools that is a variable and a condition per topic. Written with a list it is the same two rows on every option, and adding a seventh topic costs nothing.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.remembering.watchChangeSomething.title = Watch it change something

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.remembering.watchChangeSomething.body = Export and go through. Check the wallpaper is locked, have the conversation that sets your variable, then check again. That before-and-after is the whole point of state, and it is the one thing no preview can show you.

[Tutorial: Putting a value into a line]

# Tutorials: a tutorial's name
tutorial.valuesInText.title = Putting a value into a line

# Tutorials: the line under a tutorial's name
tutorial.valuesInText.summary = Show a variable in dialogue, and use one in place of a typed-in value.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.valuesInText.fourWaysTheyNot.title = Four ways, and they are not interchangeable

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.valuesInText.fourWaysTheyNot.body = You have a variable. So far it can only gate things — a line plays or it does not. It can also be SHOWN, and used in place of values you would otherwise type.\n\nThere are four notations and each works in one place:\n\n[PV:name] puts one of YOUR variables into dialogue text or a button label. {PC}, {M}, {D} and {B} put the GAME's names into dialogue text. $name uses a variable's value in an action or condition box instead of a fixed one. {item} stands for the current value in a rule that repeats over a list.\n\nUsing the wrong one is quiet: the text simply shows the notation you typed, because nothing was there to replace it.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.valuesInText.showVariableLine.title = Show your variable in a line

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.valuesInText.showVariableLine.body = Go to your conversation and put [PV:name] into a node's text, with the name of the variable you declared — square brackets, PV, a colon, the name, and a closing bracket, spelled exactly.\n\nThe line will read the variable's value where the token sits. That is how a character says a number back to the player, or a name the player chose earlier in your own pack.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.valuesInText.showVariableLine.hint = Select a node and add [PV:yourVariable] to its Text.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.valuesInText.namesPlayerChose.title = The names the player chose

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.valuesInText.namesPlayerChose.body = The game has names of its own, and they are not yours to set. {PC} is the player's name. The other three stand for the family, one token each:\n\n{M} is what this player calls Anna. {D} is what they call Josef. {B} is what they call Adrian.\n\nEach player chose those words at the start, so they differ from save to save — which is the whole reason the tokens exist.\n\nSo write {M} rather than Mom, or Anna. A line that spells it out says that one word to everyone, including the player who chose something else, and they cannot tell your line from the game losing their choice. Validate warns about it if you forget.\n\nThose four are the ones known to work. What makes a braced word one of these is not documented, so treat any other as unsupported until you have watched it resolve in game.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.valuesInText.valueDoNotKnow.title = A value you do not know yet

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.valuesInText.valueDoNotKnow.body = [PV:name] SHOWS a variable to the player. $name is the other direction: it hands the variable's value to an action or a condition, in place of the number or word you would otherwise type in. Nobody reads it — it never reaches the screen.\n\nWhy that matters: a typed-in value is decided now, while you are authoring. A $name value is decided later, while the game is running, and can be different every time.\n\nMake one and watch. Declare a String variable — call it LastPropShown — and leave its default empty. Then on some node, add TWO actions on finish. The first is a Variable action, Operation Set, writing the name of the prop you put in your room into LastPropShown. The second is Set-Active with its Target typed as $LastPropShown — one dollar sign, then the variable name, no brackets and no spaces.\n\nThe second action does not name the prop. It hides whatever the first one wrote down. Change what the first action writes — from a different node, or an integration rule — and the second acts on something else without being edited.\n\nThat is the shape it earns its keep in: one action that says "put back whatever was showing" instead of one action per object that might have been. It works in condition boxes too, so a gate can compare against a value chosen at runtime.\n\nOne trap: $$ is how you write a literal dollar sign, for the rare line where you want one.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.valuesInText.valueDoNotKnow.hint = Add an action on finish, then type $ and your variable name into a value box.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.valuesInText.oneLists.title = And one for lists

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.valuesInText.oneLists.body = The fourth is for integration rules, which you will meet next. A rule can repeat over a list — every value in a List variable, or a few typed in — and {item} stands for whichever value it is running for at the time.\n\nThe name is yours: the box beside it sets the word, and item is only the default. One rule written once then does the work of six near-identical ones.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.valuesInText.whichOneGoesWhere.title = Which one goes where

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.valuesInText.whichOneGoesWhere.body = Worth keeping straight, because none of them complains when it is in the wrong place:\n\n[PV:name] — your variables, in text and button labels.\n{PC} {M} {D} {B} — the game's names, in text.\n$name — your variables, in action and condition value boxes.\n{item} — the current value, inside a repeating rule.\n\nA token in the wrong place is shown to the player exactly as you typed it. If a line reads [PV:gold] in game, that is this.

[Tutorial: Rules that run themselves]

# Tutorials: a tutorial's name
tutorial.rules.title = Rules that run themselves

# Tutorials: the line under a tutorial's name
tutorial.rules.summary = Make the pack do something without anyone talking to it.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.rules.notEverythingWaitsSpoken.title = Not everything waits to be spoken to

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.rules.notEverythingWaitsSpoken.body = Integration rules watch the game and act on their own — moving characters around, rebuilding lists, reacting to the day changing. This is where a pack stops being a set of conversations.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.rules.scenesGameSBig.title = Scenes are the game's big pictures

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.rules.scenesGameSBig.body = A scene is a full-screen CG: the art, a frame around it, and a sound when it appears. Most of what players remember about this game is scenes, so they are worth understanding properly. A pack's scenes are built switched OFF — something has to turn one on, which is what you are about to make a rule do.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.rules.makeOne.title = Make one

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.rules.makeOne.body = Press + Scene, set Sprite path to TutorialArt/Scenes/Dummy/DummyScene01.png, and change Display name off "New Scene" — that is the name a gallery shows.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.rules.makeOne.hint = + Scene, then the Sprite path and Display name rows.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.rules.frameSound.title = The frame and the sound

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.rules.frameSound.body = A new scene already has a Vanilla frame — one of the game's own borders, so your CG sits in the picture the way the rest do. Custom frame takes your own art instead. Mode decides what plays when the scene appears: the prototype carries its own audio trigger, and picking kiss or flash swaps that for one of the game's signals, while Silent strips it. Change them if you like — nothing here has to be touched.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.rules.soundComesTwoOther.title = Sound comes from two other tabs

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.rules.soundComesTwoOther.body = Music holds background tracks, and an action switches between them. SFX holds one-shot effects, played by an action the same way. Both take an OGG, WAV or MP3 from your pack folder, and both have a Play button so you can hear one without starting the game.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.rules.trickWorthKnowingAbout.title = The trick worth knowing about SFX

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.rules.trickWorthKnowingAbout.body = An effect does not need an action at all. Auto-trigger patterns is a list of words that fire it on their own whenever one of them shows up in a line, written between asterisks by convention, like *plap*. Drop extra recordings beside the first one named _1, _2 and so on and the game picks between them at random, so a sound used often does not wear thin. Neither is discoverable from the tab, which is the only reason this step exists.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.rules.addRule.title = Add a rule

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.rules.addRule.body = Use + Rule. Give it a description while you are there — in six months the conditions will still be readable and the reason will not.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.rules.addRule.hint = + Rule, in the toolbar above the list.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.rules.decideWhenFires.title = Decide when it fires

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.rules.decideWhenFires.body = OnRisingEdge fires once when the conditions start passing, which is what you want almost every time. WhilePassing fires every frame they hold — reach for it only to keep something continuously in step.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.rules.giveSomethingWatch.title = Give it something to watch

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.rules.giveSomethingWatch.body = Under Conditions, press + Add condition and gate it on the variable you made in the last tutorial. A rule with no conditions has nothing to wait for.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.rules.giveSomethingWatch.hint = + Add condition, in the Conditions box above the Actions one.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.rules.somethingDo.title = And something to do

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.rules.somethingDo.body = Under Actions, press + Add action. Set Type to Set active, Category to Scene, and Target to the scene you built, leaving Active ticked. That is what finally puts it on screen: a scene is built switched off, and this is the switch.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.rules.somethingDo.hint = + Add action, Type = Set active, Category = Scene, pick your scene.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.rules.watchThink.title = Watch it think

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.rules.watchThink.body = Tick Set for condition debugging and the game logs what this rule decided, whenever that changes. It is the fastest way to find out why a rule did nothing — and worth clearing again before you share the pack.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.rules.shape.title = That is the shape of it

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.rules.shape.body = Characters, places, conversations, art, memory and rules — and one door from the bedroom to go and stand in all of it. Three things these seven never made you build: Map Buttons, which put a place on the world map rather than on another room's strip; Music; and SFX. All three work like everything you have already done, and the Documentation section on this tab covers them field by field — start with Start here if any of it stopped making sense.

[Tutorial: A quest in the journal]

# Tutorials: a tutorial's name
tutorial.aQuest.title = A quest in the journal

# Tutorials: the line under a tutorial's name
tutorial.aQuest.summary = Make a quest that starts, moves along and finishes by itself.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aQuest.journalGameS.title = The journal is the game's

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aQuest.journalGameS.body = The game's quest screen lists the game's own quests. A quest made here goes into that same list, beside them, with the same popups when it starts and when it is finished.\n\nEverything about it is on this tab: what it is called, when it starts, its steps, and what finishes each one.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aQuest.startOne.title = Start one

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aQuest.startOne.body = Press + Quest. It arrives with a title waiting to be replaced.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aQuest.startOne.hint = + Quest, at the top of the list on the left.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aQuest.callSomething.title = Call it something

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aQuest.callSomething.body = Give it a title. The game writes its own as a name and then the person it is about in brackets - "Secrets (Adrian)" - and the journal paints the bracketed part pink by itself. Yours can follow that or not.\n\nThe runtime name under it follows the title while the quest is new. The game files a player's progress under it, which the last step comes back to.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aQuest.callSomething.hint = Title, in the Quest box on the right.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aQuest.sayWhenStarts.title = Say when it starts

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aQuest.sayWhenStarts.body = Add a start condition - any of the conditions a dialogue or a rule can use. The quest starts by itself the moment all of them pass: a variable reaching a value, a day going by, the player being somewhere.\n\nAn empty list is the one place where empty does not mean 'always'. With no start conditions a quest waits for a Quest action to start it, so a quest you have only begun writing never turns up in a player's journal.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aQuest.sayWhenStarts.hint = + Add condition, in the Starts when box.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aQuest.giveSteps.title = Give it steps

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aQuest.giveSteps.body = Add two tasks and write a line for each - the lines the player reads under the quest.\n\nTasks run top to bottom. Starting the quest starts the first one, finishing one starts the next, and finishing the last completes the quest. The journal shows each one as it starts.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aQuest.giveSteps.hint = + Task twice, then select each one and fill in its Text.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aQuest.sayFinishesStep.title = Say what finishes a step

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aQuest.sayFinishesStep.body = Select a task and add a condition under Completes when. While the task is in progress, the moment its conditions all pass it is done and the next one starts.\n\nWhen it completes is the other half: actions that run as the task finishes - set a variable, show something, start another quest. They run however the task was finished, so they are the place for anything that should always follow it.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aQuest.sayFinishesStep.hint = Select a task, then + Add condition under Completes when.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aQuest.stepsInsideSteps.title = Steps inside steps

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aQuest.stepsInsideSteps.body = A task can have subtasks. The journal draws them under it with a small arrow, and never with a tick - even the game's finished subtasks look the same as unfinished ones. Only a top-level task gets the check mark.\n\nOnce a task has subtasks it finishes through them - in order, in any order, or when any one is done, as its Completes box says - so its own conditions and actions go away and its subtasks get them instead. Set it to 'by action' and its subtasks never start at all: they become notes under it, which is how the game gives hints.\n\nA task can also count - three flowers found, five photos taken. Quest actions can set the count, or it can follow a number variable you already keep. Either way it finishes the task when it reaches the target, and only shows beside a top-level task.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aQuest.conversationToo.title = From a conversation, too

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aQuest.conversationToo.body = A dialogue node, a rule or a UI button can move a quest as well, with the Quest action: start it, complete the step the player is on, add to a count. The QuestState and QuestCounter conditions ask where a player has got to, so a conversation can wait until a quest is in progress. Both can name the game's own quests too.\n\nThe game only finishes a step that is in progress, so completing the second task before the first is done does nothing at all.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aQuest.beforeRelease.title = Before you release it

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aQuest.beforeRelease.body = The game saves a player's progress on a quest under its runtime name, and on a task under the task's. Renaming either once players have your pack starts that quest over for every one of them, because to the game it is a different quest.\n\nRename freely while you are writing. After release, leave the names alone and change the title and text instead - those are only what the journal shows.

[Tutorial: A screen of your own]

# Tutorials: a tutorial's name
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.title = A screen of your own

# Tutorials: the line under a tutorial's name
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.summary = Build a panel, put something on it, and see it against the game's canvas.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.screensPackOwns.title = Screens the pack owns

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.screensPackOwns.body = This tab is for anything drawn on top of the game rather than in it — a window, a panel, a bar in the corner. Two kinds live here together, and deliberately so: screens you invent, and screens the game already has that your pack changes.\n\nThey share a tab because from your side they are the same job. A tree of objects on the left, what the selected one is underneath, and a picture of the result on the right.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.startOne.title = Start one

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.startOne.body = Press + UI. That gives you a blank panel — the plainest shape there is, and the one to start from when you do not yet know what you are making. The arrow beside the button offers the others: a window with a title bar and a close button, a dialog that asks something, a list, a tooltip.\n\nIt arrives with a name and an id already filled in. The id is what everything else in the pack uses to point at this screen, so it stays put even when you rename the thing.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.startOne.hint = + UI, at the top of the list on the left.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.callSomething.title = Call it something

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.callSomething.body = Change the Name to something you will recognise in a month — "Quest note" rather than "Blank panel 2". This is the name you will be picking out of a list every time something needs to open it.\n\nThe id underneath does not change with it, and should not: renaming a screen must never quietly unhook whatever was opening it.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.callSomething.hint = The Name box, top of the middle column.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.putSomething.title = Put something on it

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.putSomething.body = The tree is what the screen is made of. Select the panel and press + Object to put an empty object inside it; the arrow beside that button offers ready-made pieces instead — a button, a row, a line of text.\n\nEverything is inside something else, and that is what decides where it sits: an object is positioned against its parent, so moving the panel takes everything on it along.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.putSomething.hint = Select the panel in the tree first — + Object adds INSIDE whatever is selected.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.say.title = Say what it is

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.say.body = With the new object selected, the box underneath is everything about it: its name, where it sits, how big it is, and what it draws.\n\nPosition is measured from the object's anchors, in canvas pixels, and the canvas is the whole screen. Give it a name and a size you can see — a few hundred pixels each way is a good start, since anything smaller is hard to find in the preview.

# Tutorials: the nudge shown when a step has been waiting a while
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.say.hint = Select the object you just added, then fill in Name and Size.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.pictureTelling.title = What the picture is telling you

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.pictureTelling.body = The preview draws your screen against the game's own canvas at the size the game uses, with the selected object picked out. It is not an approximation of the layout — it is the layout, which is why an object off the edge here is off the edge in the game.\n\nA screen built on one of the game's own shows that screen underneath your changes, so you can see what you are altering rather than guessing at it.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.makingAppear.title = Making it appear

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.makingAppear.body = A screen you build starts switched off, the same way a scene does. Something has to turn it on, and it is the action you already know: Set Active, with its category set to UI and your screen named as the target.\n\nPut that on a dialogue node, or in a rule, or on a button of your own — anywhere an action can go. Switching it off again is the same action with Active unticked.

# Tutorials: the heading of a step
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.changingOneGameAlready.title = Changing one the game already has

# Tutorials: what the step says
tutorial.aScreenOfYourOwn.changingOneGameAlready.body = + Vanilla is the other half of this tab. Choose one of the game's screens and the tree fills with what is actually in it, ready to be moved, hidden, relabelled or added to.\n\nOnly your differences are stored. An object you never touch is not in your pack at all, which is why Reset on an object makes it stop being a change and drop out — and why a pack that alters one label on the shop stays a pack that alters one label, rather than a copy of the shop that will not survive the game being patched.

[Validation]

# Validation message; {word} is a word like mom, {token} the code that says it the player's way
validation.dialogue.kinWord = This line writes "{word}". Players choose what they call their family, so {token} says it back the way they picked it — writing the word says it to everyone, including whoever chose something else.

# A text typed only in a translation: the pack has no words of its own for it. {own} is the pack's language, {languages} the translations that have it, {first} the one other players get.
validation.text.onlyInTranslation = Has no text in the default language ({own}), only in {languages}. A player reading any other language gets the {first} text - write its default text.

# A line that does not fit the game's dialogue box even at the smallest size it shrinks to. {language} is the language of the words measured.
validation.dialogue.lineTooLong = Too long for the game's dialogue box in {language}: even at its smallest size it runs out of the box and over whatever is below it. Split it into two lines.

# A default text that plainly reads as another language. {language} is that language's own name, {own} the pack's.
validation.text.looksLike = Looks like {language}, but the pack says it is written in {own} - so every player without a translation into their language reads it like this. If you meant to translate it, switch to that language in the list at the top of the window and type it there. If the whole pack is in {language}, change Written in on the ModForge tab.

validation.text.notOwnLanguage = Is not in the alphabet {own} is written in, but the pack says it is written in {own} - so every player without a translation into their language reads it like this. If you meant to translate it, switch to that language in the list at the top of the window and type it there. If the whole pack is in another language, change Written in on the ModForge tab.

# Most of the pack reads as another language than the one it says it is written in.
validation.pack.looksLike = Most of this pack looks like {language}, but it says it is written in {own}. Change Written in on the ModForge tab to the language it is written in: translations are made from it, and players without a translation are given it.

validation.pack.notOwnLanguage = Most of this pack is not in the alphabet {own} is written in, but it says it is written in {own}. Change Written in on the ModForge tab to the language it is written in: translations are made from it, and players without a translation are given it.

# Validation message
validation.dialogue.choiceOptionOnStart.one = This node is one of a Choice's options and has {count} action on start. When an option counts as STARTED is not something the pack can rely on — it may be when the menu is drawn rather than when the player picks it, in which case these run for options nobody chose. Move them to Actions on finish, which happens once the option has been taken.
validation.dialogue.choiceOptionOnStart.other = This node is one of a Choice's options and has {count} actions on start. When an option counts as STARTED is not something the pack can rely on — it may be when the menu is drawn rather than when the player picks it, in which case these run for options nobody chose. Move them to Actions on finish, which happens once the option has been taken.

# Validation message
validation.dialogue.choiceWithoutOptions = This node is a Choice with no children, so it is a menu offering nothing to pick. If it is meant to be a line, set Kind to Text. Older packs collect these on their own: adding a node with + Child or + Sibling used to copy the kind of the node it came from, so every option under a Choice was saved as a Choice as well.

# Validation message; {name} is a variable's name
validation.text.unknownPackVar = Text uses [PV:{name}], but no variable called '{name}' is declared in this pack. An undeclared name reads as an empty string, so the token disappears from the line rather than showing up as a mistake.

# Validation message
validation.text.malformedToken = Text has a [PV: that never closes with a ]. The token only counts when it ends in a square bracket, so this one is shown to the player exactly as it was typed.

# Validation message; {name} is what the action names
validation.action.unknownSfx = '{name}' is not an effect in this pack. A runtime name follows its display name, so renaming one leaves references behind.

# Validation message; {name} is what the action names
validation.action.unknownScene = '{name}' is not a scene in this pack. A runtime name follows its display name, so renaming one leaves references behind.

# Validation message; {art} is a file, {target} the category the action targets
validation.action.animatedSpriteCategory = '{art}' is animated, and animation is only supported for the Scenes category — this action targets '{target}'. Point it at a scene, or use a still image here.

# Validation message; {art} is a file
validation.action.animatedSpriteNoTarget = '{art}' is animated, and animation is only supported for the Scenes category — this action targets nothing. Point it at a scene, or use a still image here.

# Validation message
validation.pack.idMissing = packId is required

# Validation message
validation.character.nameMissing = Character name is required

# Validation message
validation.outfit.keyMissing = Outfit key is required

# Validation message
validation.outfit.duplicateKey = Duplicate outfit key '{key}'

# Validation message
validation.outfit.gameObjectNameMissing = gameObjectName is required

# Validation message
validation.outfit.unknownVanillaBust = '{name}' isn't a bust in the 1.8E catalog — the runtime will still look for it under 2_Bust_Manager, but check the name

# Validation message
validation.outfit.overrideNoSlot = A replaced texture has no slot, so nothing will be replaced

# Validation message
validation.outfit.overrideDuplicate = '{texture}' is replaced twice — only one of them will be used

# Validation message

# Validation message
validation.condition.randomPerFrame = 'Random' re-rolls on every evaluation, and this condition is re-checked every frame (~60×/sec), so its chance doesn't mean what it says. Replace with 'DailyChance' (rolls once per in-game day), or a LevelRandom variable + a numeric comparison (once per visit). 'Random' is fine on a dialogue NODE's conditions or a level hook, which are evaluated once.

# The first choice: nothing changes. The default
quests.stepsAdded.leave = Leave it finished

# Under the first choice
quests.stepsAdded.leave.note = It stays finished, and they don't see the new tasks. The safest choice.

# The second choice: the quest is in progress again at the new tasks
quests.stepsAdded.reopen = Reopen at the new steps

# Under the second choice
quests.stepsAdded.reopen.note = The quest is in progress again, with every task they did still done and only the new ones left. Only when all the new tasks come after the ones the quest had, and they had done every one of those; otherwise it stays finished, so nobody is stuck behind a task they can't reach.

# The third choice: back to not started
quests.stepsAdded.startOver = Start it over

# Under the third choice
quests.stepsAdded.startOver.note = Their progress on it is cleared and it goes back to not started, so it starts again the way it always starts. Each task's actions run again as it is done.

Close it and run Options ▸ Check for updates now again, or unpack the plugin zip over your game folder by hand. Until then the two are out of step, and a pack written against one can fail against the other.

# the English. Not a fault on its own - see the second sentence

# Checking a translation file

# Checking a translation file; {forms} are names like one, few, many

# Checking a translation file; part of the line below

[Pack translations: translating by machine]

# Said against one line that came back damaged. The line is not written and the
# pack's own words are kept, so these explain why a line was left alone.
packText.mt.lostCodes = The translation came back without {codes}.

# The braces, tags and tokens are named without writing any of them out, since
# writing one here would make it a gap in this text rather than words about one.
packText.mt.differentCodes = The translation does not carry the same words in braces, tags and tokens as the original.

packText.mt.empty = The translation came back empty.

# Said when a whole run stops. Each explains what to do next, because stopping
# is not a failure: everything already translated is kept.
packText.mt.miscounted = The translation service sent back a different number of lines from the number it was given, so nothing in that batch could be matched up to the text it came from.

packText.mt.refused = The translation service refused the request ({status}): {why}

packText.mt.noAnswer = The translation service would not answer. Everything translated so far is kept - run it again later and it carries on from where it stopped.

packText.mt.timedOut = The translation service did not answer in time.

packText.mt.unreadable = The translation service answered with something ModForge cannot read. It may have changed, or it may be asking for a captcha in a browser.

packText.mt.tooMany = Too many requests in too short a time. Leave it a while and run it again - everything already translated is kept.

packText.mt.blocked = The translation service refused this connection. It may have blocked this address for a while, or it may be asking for a captcha in a browser.

packText.mt.answered = The translation service answered {status}.

# Said when too many lines in a row fail the markup check: the engine is answering but not translating.
packText.mt.notWorking = The translation service is answering, but {damaged} of the last {total} lines came back damaged - so it has probably changed, rather than having trouble with a few lines. The run stopped here; everything translated before this is kept.

# Said when the test sentence sent before a run does not come back properly translated.
packText.mt.probeFailed = The translation service did not translate a test sentence properly, so nothing of the pack was sent. It may have changed how it answers. Try again later, or use another translation engine.

# Said when Google answers with its block page rather than a translation. Not a slow-down: asking again only makes it last longer, so the run stops at once.
packText.mt.captcha = Google has stopped answering this connection for now - it says your network may be sending automated queries, and wants a captcha solved in a browser. It usually lifts on its own after a while, so try again later. Everything already translated is kept.

# The title of the small window that shows the Google Translate web page while a pack is translated through it
packText.web.title = Translating through the Google Translate website

# At the top of that window
packText.web.explain = ModForge is translating your pack through this page, because Google's free translation service is not answering as it should. The page is locked while ModForge works it - leave the window open; it closes by itself when the translation is done. If Google asks you something here, the translation stops and the page unlocks for you to answer; nothing here is answered for you.

# Said while a run changes over to the web page
packText.web.switching = Google's free translation service did not translate the test sentence properly. Trying the Google Translate website instead...

# Above a run's report, when the web page was used
packText.web.used = Translated through the Google Translate website, because Google's free translation service did not pass the test sentence.

# Added to the free service's failure when the web page failed too. {why} is what went wrong with the web page
packText.web.alsoFailed = The Google Translate website did not work either: {why}

# The web page needs Windows' own browser control, which a few computers lack
packText.web.noRuntime = The Google Translate website could not be opened, because Microsoft Edge WebView2 is not installed on this computer. It comes with Windows 11, and Microsoft offers it as a free download.

# {why} is Windows' own reason
packText.web.couldNotOpen = The Google Translate website could not be opened ({why}).

# The person closed the web page's window while a run was going
packText.web.closed = The translation window was closed, so the run stopped. Everything already translated is kept.

# Google shows a captcha or its unusual-traffic page in the web page's window. The run stops; the window stays for the person to see
packText.web.blocked = Google stopped translating and is showing a check in the translation window. ModForge does not answer those, so the run stopped here. Everything already translated is kept - try again later.

# Google asks a question (its consent page) before translating. The run stops; the window stays
packText.web.consent = Google is asking something in the translation window before it translates. Answer it there yourself if you want to, then run the translation again. Everything already translated is kept.

# {code} is the language's code, such as pt-BR
packText.web.language = The Google Translate website does not know the language code {code}, so nothing was translated into it.

# The web page did not show a translation within half a minute
packText.web.timedOut = The Google Translate website did not show a translation in time.

# Added to the top of a file a machine has written into, so whoever opens it
# knows what they are looking at before they read a word of it.
packText.mt.fileNote = Some of the lines below were translated by a machine and nobody has checked them. Correcting one is as simple as writing over it: a line that already says something is never translated again.

[Translate the pack]

# The window's own title.
translatePack.title = Translate this pack

# The bold line at the top.
translatePack.headline = Translate everything a player reads into the languages you tick.

# The grey paragraph under it. Said before the button, not after: handing a
# pack's text to somebody else's service is a decision, and the rate limit
# lands on the author's own connection.
translatePack.warning = Your pack's text is sent to Google's free translation service. It is not an official one: it can refuse, it can block your connection for a while if asked too often, and it can change without warning. If it stops working, ModForge tries the Google Translate website instead, in a small window you can see. Nothing is sent while anyone is playing - the translation is written into your pack and stays there. Everything a machine writes is marked, and you can correct any of it by writing over it.

# The tick box under the warning. Off unless ticked.
translatePack.names = Translate character names too

# Under that box: what happens with it off, and why it starts off. The example is a name that is also an ordinary word; any such name in your language will do
translatePack.names.warning = Left unticked, every character's name stays as it is written - spelled in their own alphabet for languages that have one - in the name itself and wherever a line mentions it with a capital letter. Ticked, names are translated like any other text, and a translator reads a name as the word it looks like: a character called Hope can come back as Hoffnung in German, in every line that mentions her.

# The heading over the list of names, when a ticked language uses another alphabet
translatePack.spellings = Names in other alphabets

# The grey text at the top of that list
translatePack.spellings.help = These languages write names in an alphabet of their own. Type how each name is written, or press Translate to have Google suggest spellings first - then check every suggestion, because it can still come back as an ordinary word rather than a name. A name left empty stays in Latin letters.

# The heading over the column of names
translatePack.spellings.name = Name

# While Google is asked for spellings
translatePack.spellings.asking = Asking Google how the names are spelled...

# After it has answered. Nothing has been translated yet
translatePack.spellings.check = Google's suggestions are in the list of names, in italics. Check each one and correct any that came back as an ordinary word rather than a name, then press Translate again.

# When it could not answer. The reason it gave is shown on the line under this
translatePack.spellings.failed = Google could not suggest spellings, and nothing has been translated. Press Translate again later and it will ask again - or type the names in the list yourself first; a name left empty stays in Latin letters.

# Under the line above, one per language Google answered but no spelling could be read out of. {language} is the language's own name; {answer} is what Google sent back, as it was
translatePack.spellings.unread = {language}: no spelling could be read out of Google's answer - type those names yourself. Google answered: {answer}

# Tooltip on a suggested spelling not yet checked
translatePack.spellings.suggested.tip = Suggested by Google - check it before translating.

# Over the list of languages, above Google's own check page, when Google stops to make sure a person is asking
translatePack.check = Google wants to check that a person is asking before it translates any more. Answer its check below - ModForge never answers it for you - and the translation carries on by itself. Stop ends it here, and everything translated so far is kept.

# While the check is showing
translatePack.check.waiting = Waiting for you to answer Google's check...

# Once it has been answered
translatePack.check.passed = Check passed - carrying on.

# When Google's page, shown to the person, has no check on it - only "try again later"
translatePack.check.none = Google's page has nothing to answer - it only says to try again later - so ModForge carries on through the Google Translate website instead, which shows a check you can answer if it wants one.

translatePack.languages = Languages

# Beside a language the pack has no file for at all.
translatePack.notStarted = not started - {count} texts to translate

# Beside one where everything is translated.
translatePack.finished = fully translated

# Beside one part-way through.
translatePack.partly = {missing} of {total} still to translate

# When ModForge has no translations of its own to offer.
translatePack.noLanguages = ModForge has no other languages installed, so there is nothing to translate into yet.

translatePack.selectAll = Select all
translatePack.selectNone = Select none
translatePack.translate = Translate
translatePack.close = Close

# The Close button while a run is going.
translatePack.stop = Stop

# When Translate is pressed with nothing ticked.
translatePack.pickOne = Tick at least one language first.

# While it runs.
translatePack.progress = {language}: {done} of {total} translated...

# While it runs, over the bar for the whole run: every language picked, counted in texts. {number} is the language it is on, {count} how many languages were picked
translatePack.progress.all = All languages: {done} of {total} texts (language {number} of {count})

# Over the Translate window's bar once a run is over, having done every language picked.
translatePack.over.finished = Translation finished.

# Over the Translate window's bar when a run was stopped, or failed, before the end.
translatePack.over.stopped = Translation stopped before the end. Everything translated so far is kept, and Translate picks up from there.

# One line per language afterwards.
translatePack.did = {language}: {count} translated.

# Added to that line when some came back unusable.
translatePack.damaged = {count} were left in the default text because the translation lost a gap, a tag or a token.

translatePack.nothingDone = Nothing was translated.
